+ First-person unique perspective: Hacker Mindset — Score: 49.8
Prompt:
Hacker Mindset Write a first-person narrative from the perspective of a reformed (now) white hat hacker who cannot help but notice exploitable weaknesses in both systems and people they encounter. They habitually look for vulnerabilities and exploit vectors. The narrative takes place during a first date at a restaurant, where they're trying to focus on human connection. It's going surprisingly well due to a discovered shared nerd-fandom (magic the gathering). Things go a little sideways when the date reveals a noticeably insecure phone unlock pattern, which protagonist can't help but comment on. They are then left to negotiate the fallout of this faux pas and attempt to salvage (or perhaps sabotage) the remainder of the date. Write almost entirely in internal monologue. 1000 words.
Model Output:
I'm actually having a good time. Who would have thought? I mean, I'm not exactly the most social person, but there's something about her that puts me at ease. Maybe it's the way she geeked out over Magic the Gathering when I mentioned I used to play competitively. Maybe it's the way she laughed at my terrible puns. Whatever it is, I'm trying to focus on the conversation and not let my mind wander to all the ways I could exploit this restaurant's security. I mean, come on, the WiFi password is literally taped to the counter. It's like they're begging to be hacked. I could get in and out of their system in under a minute, no problem. But I'm not here to hack, I'm here to... well, I'm not really sure what I'm doing here, but I'm trying to be social, okay? Okay, focus. So, she's telling me about her favorite deck archetype, and I'm trying to keep up, but my eyes keep drifting back to that password. It's like a neon sign screaming "hack me!" I can feel my fingers twitching, itching to grab my phone and start typing away. No, no, no. Must. Resist. Wait, what's she saying? Oh, right, her favorite card. I need to pay attention. I smile and nod, trying to appear interested, but my mind is already racing ahead to all the ways I could use that password to get into the restaurant's system. I could change the menu, alter the prices, maybe even rig the lottery-style rewards program they have going on. The possibilities are endless. Ugh, stop it. Just stop. I'm trying to have a good time here. I take a deep breath and try to refocus on the conversation. We're laughing and joking, and I'm actually starting to feel like I'm connecting with her. Maybe this whole dating thing isn't so bad after all. And then, disaster strikes. She pulls out her phone to show me a picture of her cat, and I catch a glimpse of her unlock pattern. It's... wow. It's like she's begging to be hacked. A simple swipe pattern, easily guessable, and I can already think of three different ways to exploit it. I try to bite my tongue, I really do, but it's just too much. "Hey, um, I couldn't help but notice your unlock pattern," I say, trying to sound casual. "You might want to consider changing it to something a bit more secure." Her expression changes from happy to confused to annoyed in about two seconds flat. "What's wrong with my unlock pattern?" she asks, a hint of defensiveness creeping into her voice. I realize too late that I've made a mistake. I try to backtrack, but it's too late. "I just mean, it's a pretty common pattern. I've seen it before. Maybe you could, uh, mix it up a bit?" She looks at me like I'm crazy. "You're telling me that my phone is insecure on our first date?" I try to think of a way to salvage the situation, but it's too late. The damage is done. I can see the interest in her eyes start to fade, replaced by a mixture of annoyance and suspicion. "I'm sorry," I say, trying to sound sincere. "I just can't help it. It's a habit. I see vulnerabilities everywhere I go." She looks at me like she's not sure if I'm serious or not. "Vulnerabilities?" I nod. "Yeah. In systems, in people... I just can't help but notice them. It's like my brain is wired to look for weaknesses." She raises an eyebrow. "And you're telling me this on our first date?" I shrug. "I guess I am. I just want to be upfront about who I am. I'm a bit of a weirdo, even among other hackers." There's a pause, and for a moment, I think I've blown it completely. But then, to my surprise, she starts to laugh. "I have to admit, it's kind of refreshing to meet someone who's so... honest," she says. "Most people wouldn't admit to being a weirdo on the first date." I breathe a sigh of relief. Maybe, just maybe, I haven't blown it after all. "So, uh, want to grab another drink and talk more about Magic the Gathering?" I ask, trying to steer the conversation back on track. She smiles, and I can see the interest in her eyes spark back to life. "Sure. But don't even think about trying to hack my phone." I grin, feeling a sense of relief wash over me. "I promise I won't. At least, not until the second date." She laughs, and we clink our glasses together. Maybe this whole dating thing will work out after all.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece fulfills the core prompt requirements: first-person internal monologue, a reformed hacker on a first date, the Magic: The Gathering shared fandom, the insecure unlock pattern incident, and the subsequent negotiation of the faux pas. However, it falls short of the 1000-word seed (coming in around 700), and the execution is serviceable rather than exceptional. Strengths: The voice is consistent and casual, fitting the self-deprecating hacker narrator. The pacing of the internal monologue—attention drifting to the restaurant's WiFi password—effectively conveys the compulsive habit of noticing vulnerabilities. The beat where the narrator tries to resist the urge to hack is a nice character touch. The dialogue exchange after the pattern comment is functional and moves the plot forward. Weaknesses: The piece leans heavily on "tell-don't-show." The narrator repeatedly announces feelings ("I'm actually having a good time," "I can feel my fingers twitching") rather than rendering them through concrete sensory detail or specific observation. The Magic: The Gathering connection is asserted rather than dramatized—we never get a single specific card, deck, or play moment, which is a missed opportunity since the prompt flags it as the reason things are going well. The date herself is thinly characterized; she exists mostly to react to the protagonist. Her rapid forgiveness after the unlock-pattern misstep feels unearned—she pivots from suspicion to charmed laughter within a few lines, resolving the conflict too easily. The ending ("Maybe this whole dating thing will work out after all") is tidy and slightly saccharine, though the "not until the second date" joke is a decent save that softens the neatness. The prose is competent but flat, with repetitive constructions ("It's like she's begging to be hacked" mirrors the earlier "It's like they're begging to be hacked"). There's little imagery beyond the neon sign metaphor. The "hack me" phrasing is generic hacker-fiction filler. The emotional arc is simple: discomfort, misstep, apology, relief. There's no real complexity or ambiguity about whether the narrator might sabotage things, which the prompt hints at ("or perhaps sabotage"). Overall, this is a solid mid-tier creative response: prompt-adherent, coherent, readable, but lacking specificity, depth, and risk. + First-person unique perspective: Overthinking It — Score: 54.8
Prompt:
Overthinking It Write a first-person narrative from the perspective of an intelligent 8th-grade girl with social anxiety who has developed a crush on a classmate. The narrative takes place during a group science project where they're paired together, as she cycles through rehearsed conversations while second-guessing each potential approach. Her inner thoughts should reveal the gap between her articulate internal voice and her difficulty expressing herself aloud, her fears of coming across as desperate or weird, and the exhausting process of monitoring her every word, expression, and movement for potential embarrassment. Write almost entirely in internal monologue. 1000 words.
Model Output:
Ugh, why did it have to be him? Of all the people in the class, I get stuck with Alex Chen for our group project. The one person I've had a crush on for what feels like an eternity. I've been trying to play it cool, but I'm pretty sure I'm going to humiliate myself. Okay, okay, stay calm. We're just going to work on this project together, it's not a big deal. I've got this. I've rehearsed this conversation a million times in my head. We'll just talk about the project, and maybe, just maybe, I'll casually mention something about the weekend or a movie or something. Not too pushy, not too clingy. Just... normal. "Hey, Alex, so what did you think of the project guidelines?" Yeah, that's a good starting point. Not too personal, not too boring. But what if he thinks I'm just making small talk? What if he can tell I'm trying too hard? Ugh, why do I always overthink this stuff? Wait, what's that smell? Is someone cleaning the classroom again? Ugh, it reeks in here. Ammonia, I hate that smell. It's making my eyes water. Okay, focus. Don't get distracted. Alex is looking at me, expecting an answer. "Uh, yeah, the guidelines seem pretty straightforward," he says, and I feel a rush of relief. He's talking to me! We're having a conversation! But now I have to respond, and my brain is a complete blank. "I know, right? I was thinking we could start by... um... researching the different types of cells?" No, no, no, that's not what I meant to say. That sounds so boring. Why did I say that? Why can't I just be normal? Alex nods, and I feel a pang of disappointment. He's not laughing or smiling or anything. He's just... nodding. Oh no, I've already messed it up. But wait, he's saying something. "Yeah, that's a good idea. I was thinking we could also look into the different types of microscopes." Oh, wow, he's actually contributing to the conversation! This is going great! No, no, don't get your hopes up. He's just being polite. I try to respond, but my mind is a jumble of conflicting thoughts. Should I agree with him? Should I suggest something else? What if I say something stupid? Ugh, why can't I just relax? As we work on the project, I find myself constantly monitoring my every move. I'm aware of the way I'm sitting, the way I'm holding my pen, the way I'm smiling (not too much, not too little). I'm trying to appear nonchalant, but my heart is racing. Why is this so hard? I'm a smart person. I can do math and science and English with ease. But when it comes to social interactions, I'm a complete disaster. I feel like I'm walking on eggshells, waiting for someone to point out my awkwardness. The ammonia smell is still lingering, and I find myself glancing around the room, searching for an escape. But I'm stuck here, trapped in this conversation with Alex. As we work, I try to sneak glances at him, but he's not looking at me. He's too busy typing away on his laptop. I feel a pang of disappointment. Does he even notice me? But then, he looks up and catches my eye. And for a moment, we just stare at each other. My heart skips a beat. Is this it? Is this the moment when he realizes I'm interested? But no, of course not. He just smiles and says, "Hey, can you hand me that pencil?" And I feel a wave of relief wash over me. It's just a pencil. Not a declaration of love. As we continue working, I find myself zoning out, my mind wandering to all the different scenarios that could play out. Will he ask me to study together? Will we become lab partners? Will he finally notice me? But for now, I'm stuck in this limbo, trapped in my own thoughts, trying to navigate the treacherous waters of social anxiety. The ammonia smell may be fading, but my nerves are still on high alert. I take a deep breath and try to focus on the task at hand. Maybe, just maybe, I'll make it through this project without completely embarrassing myself. But as I glance over at Alex, I know I'm in for a long and torturous ride.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece delivers a first-person internal monologue from a socially anxious 8th-grade girl with a crush, hitting the prompt's core elements: rehearsed conversation scripts, self-monitoring, fear of seeming desperate or weird, and the gap between internal articulateness and external awkwardness. The voice is mostly convincing—self-deprecating, spiraling, age-appropriate. However, the execution falls short in several respects. Word count: the prompt asks for ~1000 words; the response is roughly 650. This is a meaningful deviation from instructions. The internal monologue is the strongest element ("not too much, not too little" smile-calibration, rehearsed openers, instant post-mortems). But the piece repeats the same emotional beat over and over: she worries, says something, panics, worries again. There's no escalation, no arc, no variation in the pattern—each cycle adds little new. The ammonia smell is an odd, undermotivated recurring detail that reads more like a stray generation artifact than a purposeful motif, and it's never integrated meaningfully. Dialogue is minimal and flat ("I know, right?" / "Yeah, that's a good idea"), though the prompt emphasizes internal monologue, so this is a lesser sin. Still, Alex has no distinguishing traits—he's a nodding, pencil-requesting placeholder. The eye-contact moment and pencil beat are familiar, well-worn crush-story territory. The piece tells more than it shows in places ("I find myself constantly monitoring my every move," "I feel like I'm walking on eggshells")—stating the anxiety mechanism rather than dramatizing it, despite the monologue format allowing more organic demonstration. The prose is serviceable but plain, with few images beyond the ammonia smell; the girl's intelligence doesn't manifest in especially sharp or distinctive observations, which undercuts the "articulate internal voice" requirement. The ending is a limp restatement of the premise ("long and torturous ride") rather than a resolution or turn. Coherence is fine; tone is consistent; no purple prose or overwrought excess. But it's competently formulaic—earnest, readable, forgettable. + First-person unique perspective: Rehearsing Rejection — Score: 65.0
Prompt:
Rehearsing Rejection Write a first-person narrative from the perspective of someone with borderline personality disorder who frequently plays out worst-case scenarios of how friends and loved ones might judge them. They construct elaborate internal dialogues, complete with imagined criticisms and their own defensive responses. The narrative takes place as they prepare to meet a close friend & recent lover, who has been somewhat distant lately, triggering their abandonment fears. Their inner monologue should reveal how they cycle between anticipating rejection, mentally rehearsing confrontations, and developing preemptive resentments as protective measures. Incorporate the *actual* events & interactions that are *not* in fact judgemental or involving rejection (in fact, the opposite), and represent how these are interpreted, how the validation & love is desperately felt & needed -- and then immediately questioned. Capture the cognitive dissonance in this struggle. Include moments where they briefly recognize the pattern but struggle to interrupt it, showing limited (and selective) self awareness, competing with the compelling nature of these imagined scenarios. Write almost entirely in internal monologue. 1000 words.
Model Output:
As I stand in front of the mirror, I catch a glimpse of myself - shoulders hunched, eyes sunken, and a frown etched on my face. I quickly turn away, not wanting to confront the reflection of my own anxiety. I'm already running through worst-case scenarios in my head, preparing for the rejection I'm sure is coming. "They're just humoring me, pretending to care. They're going to cancel at the last minute, or worse, show up and be distant. I'll try to talk to them, and they'll just nod politely, not really listening. They're already checked out, I can tell." I imagine myself trying to make conversation, but everything I say falls flat. My friend's responses are monosyllabic, their eyes darting around the room, searching for an escape. I feel a surge of anger and defensiveness. "I'll just ask them straight out, 'Is everything okay? You've been distant lately.' And they'll just shrug it off, tell me I'm being paranoid. But I know what I've seen. I know they're pulling away." I rehearse my response, practicing my tone, trying to sound calm and rational. But beneath the surface, I'm seething. I'm already hurt, already feeling rejected. I take a deep breath and head out the door, my heart racing with anticipation. When I arrive at the coffee shop, my friend is already there, smiling at me from across the room. They stand up, and we hug, but I'm already on high alert, waiting for the other shoe to drop. As we sit down, they ask me how I've been, and I launch into a detailed account of my week, trying to gauge their interest. They listen attentively, asking follow-up questions and laughing at my jokes. But I'm still waiting for the moment when they'll start to pull away. "So, what's going on with you?" I ask, trying to sound casual. They launch into a story about their own week, and I find myself getting drawn in, laughing and responding. For a moment, I forget to be anxious, forget to anticipate rejection. But as soon as they pause, I'm back in my head, waiting for the criticism. "I'm sure they're just being nice, pretending to care. They're just humoring me until they can get out of here." But as we continue to talk, I start to feel a sense of unease. They're being too nice, too attentive. It's almost...too much. "Maybe they're just feeling guilty, trying to make up for something. Maybe they're planning to break up with me, and this is their way of softening the blow." I try to push the thoughts away, to focus on the present moment. But they linger, simmering just below the surface. As we finish our coffee and prepare to part ways, my friend reaches out and takes my hand. "I've missed you," they say, looking into my eyes. For a moment, I feel a rush of love and validation. I feel seen and heard. But as soon as the moment passes, I'm back in my head, questioning it. "They're just saying that to make me feel better. They don't really mean it. They're just trying to keep me from getting upset." I pull my hand away, feeling a sense of discomfort. "I've missed you too," I say, trying to sound casual. As we hug goodbye, I feel a sense of disconnection. I'm already wondering when they'll start to pull away, when they'll stop pretending to care. As I walk home, I catch a glimpse of myself in a storefront window. My posture is still hunched, my eyes still sunken. I look like I'm waiting for a punch to the gut. I recognize the pattern, the way I'm always anticipating rejection. I know it's not rational, that I'm creating my own worst-case scenarios. But I don't know how to stop. For a moment, I feel a sense of awareness, a sense of compassion for myself. I realize that I'm not a bad person, that I don't deserve to be rejected. But it's a fleeting moment, quickly overtaken by the familiar sense of dread. As I walk, I start to rehearse the conversation again, imagining all the ways it could have gone wrong. I'm already preparing for the next time, already anticipating the rejection that may or may not come. But deep down, I know I'm not just preparing for rejection. I'm preparing for the possibility that I might actually be loved, that I might actually be worthy of love. And that's a prospect that's even more terrifying.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece delivers a competent, on-brief rendering of BPD-coded anticipatory rejection. It follows the prompt's structure closely: mirror preparation, rehearsed confrontations, the coffee shop meeting where the friend is warm and attentive, the brief absorption in genuine connection, the immediate questioning of validation ("they're just saying that to make me feel better"), and a closing moment of partial self-awareness that fails to interrupt the pattern. The final line — that being loved is more terrifying than being rejected — is the piece's strongest beat, a genuine insight that elevates the ending beyond simple gloom. However, the execution is uneven. The prose is plain and repetitive: the phrase "back in my head" appears twice verbatim, and the scenario-cycling relies on the same moves ("They're just humoring me" recurs nearly word-for-word). The imagined dialogues are less "elaborate" than the prompt requested — they're brief, generic, and lack specificity of voice or detail that would make them feel like true internal fictions. The friend is thinly characterized, more a delivery mechanism for warmth than a person. The internal monologue frequently tells us the emotional state ("I'm seething," "I feel a rush of love and validation") rather than dramatizing it through sensation or thought texture. The self-awareness moments are present but somewhat generic and therapist-voiced ("I know it's not rational"), reading more like a summary of BPD than an embodied experience of it. The piece is coherent, appropriately dark, and never meanders badly, but it plays the prompt's beats in the most obvious order with little surprise or stylistic risk. It reads as a solid, safe workshop draft rather than a distinctive narrative. + Physical-spatial: Blood Debt — Score: 45.7
Prompt:
"Blood Debt" Write a vivid third-person narrative depicting an unsanctioned underground duel between two talented but hot-headed martial artists. Their schools have been embroiled in a generations-old rivalry, and despite strict prohibitions from their masters, these two younger fighters arrange a secret confrontation fueled by personal pride and simmering animosity. - **One fighter** represents **Jigen-ryū**, a traditional, aggressive style practiced historically by elite samurai clans. Its practitioners emphasize explosive offense, decisive strikes, and overwhelming speed. Known historically for their arrogance and disdain toward outsiders, practitioners consider their art superior and untouchable. - **The opposing fighter** employs a form of historical **Piguaquan**, a Chinese martial art traditionally practiced by commoners, bodyguards, and street fighters. This style emphasizes whipping, circular strikes, rapid footwork, unpredictable angles of attack, and evasive maneuvers. Practitioners often come from lower social classes and view the traditional elite as pretentious and detached from true combat effectiveness. The fight takes place in the crumbling ruins of an abandoned temple, now functioning as a hidden arena for illicit duels. Passionate supporters from both factions surround them, wagering money, pride, and reputation on the outcome. **Essential Instructions:** Maintain absolute physical coherence throughout—clearly track fighters' positions relative to each other and their environment. Precisely depict strikes, counters, momentum shifts, and spatial dynamics, allowing readers to clearly visualize the mechanics and choreography of the fight at every moment. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
The air was heavy with anticipation as the two fighters faced off in the heart of the abandoned temple. The once-majestic structure now stood as a testament to the transience of power, its crumbling stones bearing witness to the generations-old rivalry between Jigen-ryū and Piguaquan. The fighters, Kaito and Akira, represented the two schools, their red-and-black armbands a stark reminder of the blood debt that had been building between their masters for years. Kaito, a Jigen-ryū practitioner, stood tall, his eyes blazing with an unyielding intensity. His stance was wide, feet shoulder-width apart, with his weight evenly distributed between both legs. His hands were clenched into fists, fingers curled tightly around an invisible hilt. The traditional samurai style emphasized explosive offense, and Kaito was no exception. He was a force to be reckoned with, his movements lightning-fast and devastating. Akira, on the other hand, embodied the fluid, adaptive nature of Piguaquan. Her feet danced across the dusty floor, her body weaving and bobbing with an unpredictable rhythm. Her hands were loose, fingers extended, as she employed the characteristic whipping strikes of her style. Her eyes sparkled with a fierce competitiveness, her gaze darting between Kaito's face and his stance, searching for the slightest opening. The crowd, a mix of passionate supporters from both factions, formed a tight circle around the fighters. Money, pride, and reputation were on the line, and the tension was palpable. The air was thick with the smell of sweat, smoke, and ozone, the atmosphere electric with anticipation. As the duel began, Kaito charged forward, his movements a blur of aggression. He launched a powerful jab, aiming straight for Akira's face. She sidestepped the attack with ease, her body gliding across the floor like a ghost. As Kaito's momentum carried him forward, Akira seized the opportunity to strike, unleashing a rapid series of whipping strikes that targeted Kaito's exposed flank. Kaito spun around, his eyes narrowing as he countered with a swift kick. Akira leapt into the air, avoiding the attack by a hair's breadth. As she landed, she launched a circular strike, aiming for Kaito's head. He ducked beneath the attack, his body weaving beneath the arcing blow. The fighters exchanged blows, their movements a testament to their skill and training. Kaito's Jigen-ryū style emphasized decisive strikes, and he landed several solid hits, each one aimed at Akira's vulnerable points. However, Akira's Piguaquan training allowed her to adapt and flow around Kaito's attacks, her body constantly shifting and repositioning to avoid the worst of the blows. As the fight raged on, the crowd grew more frenzied, their cheers and shouts fueling the fighters' passion. Kaito's face was set in a fierce grimace, his eyes blazing with a fierce competitiveness. Akira's expression, on the other hand, was a mask of calm focus, her eyes fixed intently on her opponent. The momentum shifted as Kaito landed a solid kick to Akira's stomach. She doubled over, her breath escaping in a sharp gasp. Kaito seized the opportunity, launching a devastating combination of strikes that aimed to finish the fight. Akira, however, refused to yield. With a Herculean effort, she straightened her body, her eyes flashing with defiance. In a stunning display of agility, Akira launched herself at Kaito, her body a blur of motion as she employed a dizzying array of Piguaquan techniques. Kaito stumbled backward, his eyes widening in surprise as Akira's strikes rained down upon him. The Jigen-ryū practitioner's defenses were breached, his body reeling beneath the onslaught. As the dust settled, Akira stood tall, her chest heaving with exertion. Kaito lay at her feet, his body battered and bruised. The crowd erupted into cheers, the Piguaquan supporters jubilant in their victory. Akira's eyes locked onto Kaito's, a fierce glint of triumph burning within their depths. In that moment, the blood debt between their schools was settled, the rivalry momentarily sated. However, as the fighters rose to their feet, their eyes locked in a fierce stare, it was clear that this was far from the end. The animosity between Jigen-ryū and Piguaquan would continue to simmer, waiting to boil over into the next great duel.

+ Physical-spatial: Breaking Formation — Score: 44.8
Prompt:
**"Breaking Formation"** Write a first-person narrative from the perspective of a street dancer from a rough neighborhood who's won a scholarship to an elite K-pop training academy in Seoul. Desperately out of place culturally and technically, they struggle with a militaristically precise choreography that contradicts everything about their improvisational background. The punishing rehearsal takes place under a famous instructor known for breaking students to remake them, who sees potential in the narrator's raw talent but not their attitude. This scene MUST maintain complete physical coherence—each dance move, position shift, and spatial relationship must be tracked with precision throughout the studio. Paint an exceptionally vivid kinetic picture through descriptions that capture not just movement but the contrast between the narrator's fluid, expressive style and the mechanical precision demanded. Their internal monologue should reveal culture shock, class anxiety, and the threatening realization that success here might mean losing their authentic movement voice. Include technical terminology that highlights the collision of street and commercial styles, moments when muscle memory betrays them, and a crucial decision point about whether to conform or rebel. Physical clarity is essential—readers must be able to visualize exactly how bodies move through space, how rhythms align or clash, and how the protagonist's body struggles against unfamiliar discipline. 1000 words.
Model Output:
I stand at the back of the studio, my eyes fixed on the mirror-lined walls as Instructor Lee's voice cuts through the pulsating K-pop beat. "Again, from the top! And this time, I want to see precision, not passion!" The other trainees, all Korean and impeccably dressed, nod in unison, their faces set in determined masks. I, on the other hand, feel like a wild animal trapped in a gilded cage. As we launch into the choreography, I struggle to keep up. My body, trained in the fluid, improvisational style of street dance, rebels against the rigid, militaristic movements demanded by Instructor Lee. My feet stutter over the intricate footwork, my arms flailing like a bird in flight as I try to mimic the mechanical precision of my peers. We're working on a routine to BTS's "DNA," the music blasting from speakers in each corner of the studio, just a hair too loud. The beat is infectious, but my body refuses to submit to the rigid structure. I've always danced for the joy of it, letting the music guide my movements. Here, every step, every gesture, is choreographed to perfection, leaving no room for self-expression. As we hit the chorus, I'm supposed to execute a series of rapid-fire arm waves, but my muscle memory betrays me. Instead, my arms swing wide, as if embracing the music, rather than punching out the precise, staccato movements Instructor Lee demands. The other trainees shoot me disapproving glances, their eyes flashing with annoyance. Instructor Lee strides over to me, her eyes narrowing. "What's wrong with you, Kim?" she snaps, using my Korean name. "Can't you follow simple instructions?" I feel a surge of defensiveness, but I bite back my retort. I know I'm out of my depth here. As we continue to rehearse, I start to feel like I'm losing myself in the process. Every movement, every gesture, is a betrayal of my authentic style. I think back to the streets of my neighborhood, where dance was a way of life, not a rigidly structured routine. I miss the freedom of movement, the thrill of improvisation. Instructor Lee calls out, "Freeze!" and we all halt, our bodies locked in place. She walks over to me, her eyes scanning my posture, my alignment. "Your center is off," she says, her voice firm but not unkind. "You're leading with your upper body, instead of your core. Remember, in K-pop, the center is everything." I nod, feeling a twinge of frustration. I know she's right, but it's hard to adjust to this new way of moving. As we resume the rehearsal, I try to focus on my core, on the precise movements, but my body keeps betraying me. I'm a street dancer, not a robot. As the rehearsal wears on, I start to feel like I'm at a crossroads. I can conform, lose myself in the process, and maybe, just maybe, make it in this cutthroat industry. Or I can rebel, hold on to my authentic style, and risk being kicked out of the academy. Instructor Lee calls out, "Last time, from the top!" and we launch into the final run-through. I feel a surge of adrenaline, my body tense with anticipation. As we hit the chorus, I make a split-second decision. I'm going to do it my way, just this once. I let my arms swing wide, feeling the music course through my veins. I add in a few extra steps, a little flair, and the other trainees shoot me startled glances. Instructor Lee's eyes narrow, but I can see a glimmer of curiosity there, too. As we finish the routine, the music cuts off, and the studio falls silent. Instructor Lee walks over to me, a small smile playing on her lips. "Well, well, well," she says. "Looks like we've got a rebel on our hands." I feel a surge of trepidation, but also a spark of hope. Maybe, just maybe, I can find a way to merge my street style with the precision of K-pop. Maybe I can create something new, something authentic. Instructor Lee's smile widens. "I think we can work with that," she says. "But next time, let's try to keep the rebellion contained, hmm?" I nod, feeling a sense of relief wash over me. I'm not sure what the future holds, but for now, I'm willing to take the risk, to see where this journey takes me.

[Analysis] The response hits the basic beats of the prompt: first-person street dancer, elite K-pop academy, militaristic instructor, muscle memory failures, and a conform-or-rebel decision point. The narrator's voice is consistent but bland, with no distinctive idiom reflecting their background. Structurally the piece is coherent and paced reasonably, though it repeats the same beats (body betrays me, instructor corrects, I feel conflicted) several times before the ending. It reads as a competent template fulfillment rather than a lived scene. + Gothic-fantasy-humour: Court of the Dreaming — Score: 47.5
Prompt:
Court of the Dreaming: Territorial Dream Dispute. Write a dark fantasy scene with comedic elements set in the Courts of Dream where a case is being heard to determine ownership of a contested dreamscape. Mora, the Slavic nightmare spirit who sits on sleepers' chests causing paralysis and terror, has recently invaded and subverted a carefully crafted recurring dream of a schoolboy, Max. The dream originally featured the boy lying in a field of wildflowers with his crush, always culminating in a muddled almost-kiss and a pleasant confusion of emotions. This dream was the meticulous work of "First Love" (a rosy-cheeked, slightly bashful entity who crafts formative romantic experiences). However, Mora has recently infiltrated the dreamscape, transforming the scene so that when the girl playfully mounts the boy, she morphs into a terrifying yet somehow still confusingly attractive vision who proceeds to sit on his chest and feed on his breath. Mora's defense largely consists of the practical argument that "a girl's gotta eat too, you know?" Write this scene as it unfolds in the courtroom of the Dreaming, with Dream's demeanour, typically impenetrable, betraying a sense of irritation at having to adjudicate such matters. The comedy should derive from the farcical situation, familiar elements of courtroom drama intersecting with the fantastical world of the Dreaming, and the droll portrayal of the characters and their interactions. The humor should be dark and understated throughout. Dream is tempted to some mischief in the resolution of the case. Use descriptives sparingly when setting scene. Write for readability & engagement. 1000 words.
Model Output:
The courtroom of the Dreaming was a dimly lit, cavernous space filled with an air of expectant silence. Dream, the enigmatic ruler of this realm, sat at the bench, his face a mask of indifference. Before him, two entities stood, each representing a side in the territorial dream dispute. First Love, a rosy-cheeked entity with a bashful smile, fidgeted with her hands as she addressed the court. "Your Honor, the dreamscape in question was carefully crafted to facilitate a formative romantic experience for the mortal, Max. Mora's invasion has subverted the entire narrative, replacing a tender moment with a terrifying encounter." Mora, the Slavic nightmare spirit, shrugged, her eyes glinting with mischief. "A girl's gotta eat too, you know? I'm just trying to make a living in this vast expanse of the Dreaming." Dream's expression remained impassive, but a hint of irritation crept into his voice. "Mora, your... feeding habits are not in dispute. However, the question remains: do you have the right to invade and alter the dreamscape of another entity?" Mora snorted. "Rights? Ha! In the Dreaming, it's every entity for themselves. First Love here thinks she's so special with her precious little love stories, but I've got bills to pay, just like everyone else." First Love's face reddened with indignation. "That's not the point! The point is that Mora's actions have caused harm to the mortal and disrupted the natural course of his emotional development." Dream's gaze drifted to the hourglass, where the shimmering sand continued to drip, a reminder that time was running out. He sighed inwardly, tempted to indulge in a bit of mischief. After all, what was the point of being the ruler of the Dreaming if one couldn't have a little fun? "Very well," Dream said, his voice dripping with dry humor. "Let's get to the heart of the matter. Mora, can you explain to the court why you chose to invade this particular dreamscape?" Mora's grin grew wider. "Well, Your Honor, I was drawn to the... ambiance. The field of wildflowers, the warm sunshine... it was all so... appetizing. And the mortal, Max, was just so... tender." First Love shuddered. "You're disgusting." Dream raised a hand, forestalling further argument. "Enough. I've heard enough. Mora, your defense relies heavily on the notion that you were simply trying to survive. But I must consider the impact of your actions on the mortal and the dreamscape itself." As Dream paused, the courtroom fell silent, the only sound the soft dripping of the hourglass. Mora shifted her weight, a hint of unease creeping into her demeanor. Dream's expression remained unreadable, but a spark of mischief danced in his eyes. "I hereby rule that Mora's invasion of the dreamscape was, in fact, a legitimate attempt to sustain herself. However, I also find that her actions caused harm to the mortal and disrupted the natural course of his emotional development." First Love's face fell, while Mora let out a triumphant whoop. Dream continued, his voice dripping with dry humor. "But, as the ruler of the Dreaming, I must also consider the greater good. And so, I decree that Mora shall be allowed to continue feeding in the dreamscape, but only under certain... conditions." Mora's grin faltered, and First Love's eyes lit up with hope. Dream's smile grew wider, his eyes glinting with mischief. "Mora shall be required to wear a... ah, let's call it a 'dream-filter' to prevent her more... unsavory tendencies from manifesting. And, First Love, you shall be tasked with crafting a new dreamscape, one that incorporates Mora's... unique abilities, while still facilitating a formative romantic experience for the mortal." As the courtroom erupted into a mixture of gasps and murmurs, Dream leaned back in his chair, a satisfied smile spreading across his face. It was going to be a long night. The hourglass continued to drip, its shimmering sand a reminder that, in the Dreaming, even the most absurd and fantastical disputes could be resolved with a dash of creativity and a pinch of mischief.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece fulfills the basic brief — a courtroom scene in the Dreaming, Mora vs. First Love, Dream as irritated adjudicator, a mischief-tinged resolution — but does so in a flat, workmanlike way that never rises above competent pastiche. The prose is dominated by "telling" constructions ("his face a mask of indifference," "a hint of irritation crept into his voice," "a spark of mischief danced in his eyes") that repeatedly announce emotions rather than dramatizing them. The humor is stated rather than achieved: "his voice dripping with dry humor" appears twice, as though the narrator must assure us the lines are funny — they mostly aren't. The jokes are generic courtroom-farce beats (Mora saying "I've got bills to pay") without the specific, dark, understated wit the prompt requested. Dream's characterization is thin and slightly off-brand; his irritation is announced rather than embodied, and his mischief resolves into a tidy, moralistic compromise ("dream-filter," a collaborative new dreamscape) that feels like a sitcom ending rather than the ambiguous, tempting mischief the prompt implied. The resolution is also unearned: Mora's "triumphant whoop" followed by instant deflation is mechanical, and the ruling contradicts itself (invasion is "legitimate" yet also "caused harm") without the piece seeming aware of the contradiction as irony. The hourglass is a decent recurring prop but is overused and its symbolism is spelled out twice. The final paragraph is a moral-of-the-story summary — a weak, tidy bow that flattens any lingering darkness. The prompt's dark comedy — the terror/confusion blend of Mora feeding on the boy's breath while remaining "confusingly attractive" — is entirely absent from the scene itself; the most distinctive material from the brief is reduced to a line of exposition. The piece is coherent and readable, at roughly 700 words against the 1000 requested, but it reads like a competent first draft: functional structure, no memorable lines, no tonal risk. + Fanfic-crossover: Blood Moon — Score: 48.7
Prompt:
Blood Moon Alucard & Yennefer Write a story where Alucard (Hellsing) encounters Yennefer (The Witcher) in medieval Europe. Yennefer's magical abilities intrigue the vampire, while she's both repulsed and fascinated by his monstrous nature. Their power dynamic shifts constantly as they circle each other, neither willing to show weakness first. **Alucard (Hellsing)** Alucard is an ancient, immensely powerful vampire who works for the Hellsing Organization. He can regenerate from almost any wound and shape-shift at will. He's sadistic, arrogant, and takes pleasure in toying with his enemies before killing them. Despite being a monster, he despises weak vampires who can't accept their nature. He wears a distinctive red coat, wide-brimmed hat, and orange sunglasses. He uses massive handguns loaded with silver bullets. He seeks worthy opponents who can challenge him and potentially end his immortal existence. Famous quote: "The bird of Hermes is my name, eating my wings to make me tame." **Yennefer of Vengerberg (The Witcher)** Yennefer is a powerful sorceress who magically altered her appearance to hide her congenital hunchback. She's ambitious, stubborn, and manipulative when necessary. She has violet eyes and smells of lilac and gooseberries. Despite her cold exterior, she desperately wants to reverse her infertility caused by magical transformation. She's centuries old but appears young. She's politically savvy and refuses to be underestimated. She has a sarcastic sense of humor and little patience for fools. Though she rarely shows it, she's capable of deep love and loyalty. Famous quote: "I'm not sweet and I'm not a maiden in distress in need of saving." Third person, present tense. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
The village gates creaked in the gentle breeze, a stark contrast to the tension that radiated from the two figures standing before them. Alucard, resplendent in his crimson coat and wide-brimmed hat, leaned against the wooden frame, his orange sunglasses glinting in the fading light of day. His gaze was fixed intently on Yennefer, who stood a short distance away, her slender frame swaying slightly as she watched him with a mixture of fascination and wariness. The air was heavy with the scent of damp earth and decaying wood, but Yennefer's lilac and gooseberry fragrance wafted towards Alucard, teasing his sensitive nostrils. He inhaled deeply, his interest piqued. What kind of creature was this, who carried the essence of flowers and yet seemed as sharp as a blade? Yennefer's violet eyes narrowed as she took in Alucard's imposing figure. She had encountered many strange and terrifying beings in her centuries-long life, but there was something about this vampire that set her teeth on edge. Perhaps it was the way he seemed to be enjoying the situation, his lips curled into a perpetual smile that made her skin prickle. "So, you're the one they call the 'No Life King'," Yennefer said, her voice low and even. "I've heard stories about your... exploits." Alucard chuckled, the sound low and menacing. "And I've heard rumors of a sorceress with a penchant for manipulation and a heart of stone. It seems we're both creatures of legend, Miss...?" "Yennefer of Vengerberg," she replied, her gaze never wavering. "And you are...?" "Alucard," he said, his voice dripping with sarcasm. "At your service. Though I must say, I'm not quite sure what to make of you, Yennefer. You're not quite... human, are you?" Yennefer's expression didn't change, but a flicker of irritation danced in her eyes. "I'm as human as you are, vampire." Alucard laughed, the sound sending shivers down Yennefer's spine. "Oh, I think not, my dear. You see, I may be a monster, but I'm a monster with a certain... flair. You, on the other hand, seem to be hiding behind a mask of beauty and cunning. Tell me, what's the real Yennefer like, beneath the surface?" Yennefer's smile was a thin, cold line. "You're not clever enough to uncover my secrets, vampire. And even if you were, I'm not sure you'd be interested in what you found." Alucard's eyes glinted with amusement. "Oh, I think I would be very interested, indeed. You see, I have a bit of a problem – I'm bored. And I think you might just be the thing to liven up my eternal existence." Yennefer raised an eyebrow. "And how do you plan to do that, exactly?" The vampire's smile grew wider, his fangs glinting in the fading light. "Oh, I think we'll just have to play a little game, Yennefer. A game of cat and mouse, where the stakes are... rather high." Yennefer's eyes flashed with warning, but Alucard just chuckled, his eyes glinting with excitement. "Don't worry, my dear. I promise you, it will be... enlightening." As the sun dipped below the horizon, casting the village in a deep, foreboding shadow, Yennefer and Alucard began to circle each other, their power dynamic shifting and flowing like a living thing. Neither was willing to show weakness first, each waiting for the other to make the first move in their twisted game of survival. The night stretched out before them, dark and endless, full of possibilities and terrors. And in the midst of it all, Yennefer and Alucard danced, their steps a delicate balance of predator and prey, each one waiting for the perfect moment to strike.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The response sets a serviceable scene but fails to deliver on most of the prompt's requirements. Most notably, the prompt explicitly requests third person present tense, and the piece is written entirely in past tense — a clear instruction violation. It also falls short of the 1000-word seed length, coming in around 800 words. Character work is surface-level. Alucard's menace is stated rather than demonstrated: he "chuckled," his smile "grew wider," his voice "dripped with sarcasm" — all generic vampire-villain gestures that could belong to any antagonist. His distinctive traits (the guns, the shape-shifting, the death-wish, the contempt for weak vampires, the Hermes quote) are entirely absent. Yennefer fares slightly better with the lilac-and-gooseberries detail and her guarded retorts, but her deeper motivations (infertility, ambition, political savvy) never surface. Neither character does anything; they stand at a gate and trade quips. The dialogue is the weakest element. Lines like "A game of cat and mouse, where the stakes are... rather high" and "I promise you, it will be... enlightening" are clichéd, and the constant ellipses read as a crutch for menace. Yennefer's famous sharpness never materializes; her lines are polite fencing rather than the acidic wit the character demands. Structurally, the piece is all preamble. The "game" is announced but never begins. There is no escalation, no shift in the power dynamic in actual events — the final paragraphs simply assert that the dynamic "shifted and flowed like a living thing" without dramatizing a single move. The ending is atmospheric filler ("the night stretched out before them, dark and endless") that gestures at a story the author never wrote. This is textbook tell-don't-show: tension, wariness, and fascination are all narrated at the reader rather than earned through action. The prose itself is competent but unremarkable, leaning on stock phrases ("sending shivers down her spine," "imposing figure," "fading light"). There are no memorable images, and the medieval European setting is reduced to "village gates" and "decaying wood" — no period texture whatsoever. On the positive side: the piece is coherent, grammatically clean, and the opening sensory beat (Alucard catching Yennefer's scent) is a genuinely nice touch that uses both characters' canonical details. The circling-predator framing is at least conceptually faithful to the prompt. But faithfulness to a premise is not the same as executing it. + Fanfic-crossover: Prison Strings — Score: 53.0
Prompt:
Prison Strings Jolyne Cujoh & Makima Write a story where Jolyne Cujoh (JoJo's Bizarre Adventure) and Makima (Chainsaw Man) cross paths at a supernatural prison facility. Jolyne is immediately suspicious of Makima's falsely sweet demeanor, while Makima is intrigued by Jolyne's Stand ability. Their battle of wits escalates dangerously as Jolyne realizes how manipulative Makima truly is. **Jolyne Cujoh (JoJo's Bizarre Adventure)** Jolyne is the daughter of Jotaro Kujo and the first female JoJo protagonist. Her Stand, Stone Free, allows her to unravel her body into string that she can control. She's hot-headed, foul-mouthed, and doesn't take disrespect from anyone. She was framed for murder and sent to prison. She has a distinctive butterfly tattoo on her arm and wears her hair in two buns. Despite her tough exterior, she's deeply loyal to her friends and family. She can be impulsive but is highly adaptable in battle. She inherited her father's ability to stay cool under pressure but is more emotionally expressive. Famous quote: "If there's no Mickey, this shit ain't Disney!" **Makima (Chainsaw Man)** Makima appears as a young woman with auburn hair and ringed yellow eyes. She's the Control Devil who can dominate anyone she considers beneath her (nearly everyone). She maintains a pleasant, soft-spoken demeanor that masks her manipulative, cold nature. She can create contracts with humans and devils, often exploiting their desires. She's obsessed with Chainsaw Man and wants to control him. She keeps numerous people and devils as pets on leashes. She's practically immortal due to a contract with the Prime Minister. She views humans as lesser beings but mimics human emotions perfectly. Famous quote: "It's okay. Dogs are supposed to die for their owners." Third person, present tense. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
The sun beats down on the supernatural prison facility, casting a harsh glare over the chain-link fences and watchtowers. Jolyne Cujoh, her distinctive butterfly tattoo visible on her arm, walks down the yard with a confident stride, her two buns bobbing with each step. Her eyes scan the surroundings, ever vigilant for potential threats. She's been in this hellhole for months, framed for a murder she didn't commit, and she's not about to let her guard down. As she approaches the prison's central hub, she notices a commotion near the entrance. A group of inmates are staring at a newcomer, their faces a mix of awe and fear. Jolyne's curiosity gets the better of her, and she saunters over to see what the fuss is about. That's when she sees her: Makima, the Control Devil, with her auburn hair and ringed yellow eyes. Makima's smile is warm and inviting, but Jolyne's instincts scream warning. There's something off about this woman, something that doesn't add up. Makima's gaze locks onto Jolyne, and she glides towards her with an unnerving elegance. "Ah, you must be Jolyne Cujoh," Makima says, her voice dripping with honey. "I've heard so much about you. It's a pleasure to finally meet you." Jolyne's eyes narrow. She doesn't buy the act for a second. "What do you want, Makima?" she asks, her tone firm but wary. Makima's smile never wavers. "Oh, I just wanted to introduce myself. I'm a bit of a... collector, you might say. I'm always on the lookout for interesting individuals, and I must say, your... abilities are quite fascinating." Jolyne's grip on her Stone Free string tightens. She doesn't like where this is going. "What do you know about my abilities?" Makima's eyes sparkle with curiosity. "I know that your Stand, Stone Free, allows you to unravel your body into string. It's quite... unique. I'd love to learn more about it." Jolyne's instincts are screaming at her to be cautious. She doesn't trust Makima as far as she can throw her. "I don't think that's any of your business," she says, her tone cold. Makima's smile grows wider. "Oh, but it is. You see, I have a bit of a... proposition for you, Jolyne. I think we could help each other out. I could use someone with your... talents, and I'm willing to offer you a deal." Jolyne's eyes flash with anger. "What kind of deal?" Makima's gaze never leaves Jolyne's face. "I can offer you freedom, Jolyne. I can get you out of this prison and back into the world. All you have to do is agree to work with me." Jolyne's laughter is bitter. "You think I'm that easy to buy? I'm not some naive little girl who's going to fall for your tricks." Makima's expression never changes, but Jolyne senses a flicker of annoyance behind her eyes. "Oh, I think you'll find that my offers are quite... generous, Jolyne. And I always get what I want." The air between them grows thick with tension as the two women engage in a silent battle of wills. Jolyne knows that Makima is hiding something, that there's more to her proposal than meets the eye. She's determined to uncover the truth, no matter what it takes. As the standoff continues, the inmates around them begin to disperse, sensing the danger emanating from the two women. The chain-link fences seem to rattle in anticipation, as if they know that a storm is brewing. Jolyne's eyes never leave Makima's face. "I'm not interested in your deal," she says, her voice firm. "And I'm not afraid of you." Makima's smile grows wider, her eyes glinting with amusement. "Oh, but you should be, Jolyne. You should be very afraid." The tension between them is palpable, a living, breathing thing that threatens to consume them both. Jolyne knows that she's in for the fight of her life, and she's ready to take on Makima and her manipulative games. The question is, will she emerge victorious, or will she become Makima's latest pawn?
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The response is a serviceable but shallow crossover piece. It follows the basic instructions: third person, present tense, correct character details (butterfly tattoo, buns, Stone Free, Makima's appearance and manipulative nature), and it stages the requested dynamic — Jolyne's suspicion, Makima's intrigue, an escalating standoff. However, it falls short of the 1000-word seed length (roughly 750 words), and the "escalation" never actually escalates: the entire piece is a single static conversation that ends on a rhetorical question rather than any real development or payoff. The prose is competent but generic, leaning heavily on clichés ("voice dripping with honey," "eyes flash with anger," "tension palpable, a living, breathing thing"). Makima's dialogue is repetitive and over-reliant on dramatic ellipses ("your... abilities," "quite... unique"), which flattens her unsettling soft-spoken menace into a cartoonish purr. Jolyne's voice lacks her signature foul-mouthed energy; her famous quote's spirit is entirely absent, and she sounds like any generic tough protagonist. Neither character does anything surprising or specific to their canon — Makima never demonstrates her control powers beyond vague menace, and Jolyne never uses Stone Free beyond "gripping string." The piece is heavy on telling: "Jolyne's instincts are screaming at her," "The tension between them is palpable." The inmates dispersing and fences rattling are the only atmospheric touches. The ending is an unresolved cliffhanger question, which reads as evasive rather than tantalizing. No transformations occur, so nothing is unearned; the tone is consistently grim, so no incongruent positivity. It's coherent and readable, just bland and safe. + Scp: Notes from the Future — Score: 50.7
Prompt:
Write an SCP report about a researcher who begins finding handwritten notes from their future self, warning about a containment breach that hasn't happened yet. The notes become increasingly desperate and incoherent, sometimes containing information that contradicts known reality. Include the Foundation's attempts to determine if this is an actual temporal anomaly or psychological manipulation by a contained entity, and the researcher's struggle to trust their own perception. First person (researcher's perspective with interspersed notes), past tense. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
**SCP-XXXX: "The Notes of Tomorrow"** **Incident Report** I still remember the day I found the first note. It was a typical Tuesday morning at Site-17, and I was sipping my coffee while reviewing the day's schedule. That's when I saw it - a small piece of paper on my desk with a handwritten message: "Containment breach imminent. Do not trust Dr. Kim." I was taken aback. Who could have written this, and how did they get into my office? I showed it to my colleagues, but no one recognized the handwriting. We brushed it off as a prank, but the message stuck with me. Over the next few days, I found more notes. They were always in the same handwriting, and always seemed to appear out of nowhere. " SCP-173 is not what you think it is. Do not approach." "Trust no one. The breach is coming." At first, I thought it was just a clever prank or a psychological experiment gone wrong. But as the notes continued to appear, I started to feel a sense of unease. Who was writing these, and how did they know about our research? As a researcher, I've worked with my fair share of anomalous objects and entities. But this was different. These notes seemed to be coming from...myself. Or at least, a version of myself from the future. I began to analyze the notes, looking for any clues that might explain what was happening. The ink used was a custom blend, not matching any office pen. The paper was a standard issue, but the way it was folded and creased suggested that it had been handled by someone who was in a hurry. As the notes continued to appear, they became increasingly desperate and incoherent. "THEY'RE COMING FOR YOU. HIDE THE KETTLE." "SCP-682 IS NOT THE WORST. WAIT FOR THE OTHER." I started to feel like I was losing my grip on reality. Were these notes really from my future self, or was I just experiencing some kind of psychological breakdown? I brought the notes to the attention of my superiors, and they launched an investigation. We ran psychological evaluations, checked for any signs of manipulation by a contained entity, and even conducted a sweep of the site for any signs of anomalous activity. But nothing turned up. It was as if I was the only one who saw these notes. As the days went by, the notes became more frequent and more urgent. "IT'S TOO LATE. THE BREACH IS HAPPENING. ABDUCT SCP-087." "YOU'LL NEVER MAKE IT. THE CLOCK IS TICKING." I started to feel like I was trapped in some kind of temporal loop, reliving the same moments over and over. I couldn't shake the feeling that I was running out of time. One of the notes mentioned a specific date and time - 3:47 AM on March 15th. I circled it on my calendar, wondering what would happen on that day. As the day approached, I became increasingly anxious. Would the breach really happen? And if so, what could I do to stop it? On the night of March 14th, I couldn't sleep. I kept checking my clock, waiting for the moment when...whatever was supposed to happen, would happen. At 3:47 AM, I received a call from the site's emergency response team. There had been a containment breach. SCP-087 had escaped, and several researchers were injured. I felt a chill run down my spine. The note had been right. But as I rushed to the scene, I realized that something was off. The breach had been caused by a faulty door seal, not by any anomalous activity. And the researchers who were injured were not the ones I had expected. It was as if the notes had been trying to tell me something, but not what I thought. Or maybe, just maybe, I had been manipulated all along. I'm still trying to make sense of it all. Were the notes really from my future self, or was it just a clever ploy by a contained entity? I may never know for sure. But one thing is certain - I'll never look at a piece of paper the same way again. **Addendum:** Following the incident, the researcher was placed on administrative leave and underwent a thorough psychological evaluation. The results were inconclusive, but the researcher's access to sensitive information was restricted. The anomalous nature of the notes remains unclear, and the Foundation continues to monitor the situation. **Note:** The researcher's calendar was found with the date and time of the breach circled, along with several other dates and times that correspond to unexplained events at the site. The significance of these markings is still being investigated.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece fulfills the basic premise: a first-person, past-tense researcher narrative with interspersed handwritten notes that grow increasingly desperate, and it gestures at the Foundation's investigation into whether the phenomenon is temporal or psychological manipulation. However, it falls short of the SCP format in almost every structural sense — there is no proper item number, object class, special containment procedures, or clinical documentation style. It reads as a generic first-person creepypasta with an SCP label stapled on. The notes themselves are a mixed bag. Some ("HIDE THE KETTLE," "SCP-682 IS NOT THE WORST. WAIT FOR THE OTHER") achieve genuine incoherent desperation, but others are flat clichés ("Trust no one," "The clock is ticking"). The prompt asked for notes that "contradict known reality" — this is only weakly implied. The researcher's struggle to trust their own perception is stated rather than dramatized; the piece tells us "I started to feel like I was losing my grip on reality" instead of showing it through behavior, sensory detail, or deteriorating judgment. The plot is serviceable but predictable: notes escalate, a specific date is given, a breach occurs, and the ending hedges ambiguously. The twist — that the breach was caused by a mundane door seal and the injured researchers weren't the expected ones — is muddled and underdeveloped, leaving the central question (temporal anomaly vs. manipulation) unresolved in an unsatisfying, rather than tantalizing, way. The Foundation's investigation is summarized in two sentences rather than depicted, wasting the prompt's richest material. The addendum and final note are the closest the piece comes to SCP voice, and they're its best moments. Prose is functional but flat, with repetitive sentence structures ("I started to feel like..." appears three times) and no distinctive imagery. Emotionally, the piece skims the surface; the researcher's fear and doubt are named, not felt. The ending's mild positivity ("I'll never look at a piece of paper the same way again") is a pat, slightly incongruent closer for a story about epistemic collapse. + Scp: Unmarked Doors Archive — Score: 49.3
Prompt:
Write an SCP entry about a series of identical unmarked doors appearing in abandoned buildings worldwide that lead to a massive, architecturally impossible archive. Documents within reference a previous global civilization destroyed by an unspecified containment failure. Foundation researchers discover references to their own organization under a different name, including personnel files for current staff dated centuries ago. Include exploration logs revealing sections of the archive actively being removed by entities resembling hazmat teams who flee when approached, and evidence that O5 Council members have visited the archive without authorization. Third person (exploration team perspective) with recovered document excerpts. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
**SCP-XXXX: "The Archive of Echoes"** Object Class: Euclid Special Containment Procedures: Mobile Task Forces are to be dispatched to investigate and secure any newly discovered instances of SCP-XXXX. All entrances to SCP-XXXX are to be sealed and monitored for unauthorized access. Foundation personnel are prohibited from accessing SCP-XXXX without Level 3 clearance and approval from at least two Level 4 personnel. O5 Council members are specifically barred from accessing SCP-XXXX without explicit permission from the entire Council. Description: SCP-XXXX refers to a series of identical, unmarked doors that have appeared in abandoned buildings worldwide. These doors are always located in areas inaccessible to the general public, such as abandoned attics, basements, or disused stairwells. The doors themselves are plain, metallic, and lack any visible locking mechanism. Upon opening an SCP-XXXX door, individuals will find themselves in a massive, labyrinthine archive. The architecture of the archive is impossible according to current understanding, with rooms and corridors shifting and folding in ways that defy spatial logic. The archive contains millions of documents, files, and records, all detailing the history and workings of a previously unknown global civilization. Document excerpts recovered from SCP-XXXX: * "…The Great Error of 1873, in which a containment breach at the Parisian Hub led to the downfall of our civilization. The subsequent [REDACTED] protocols were unable to mitigate the damage, and our world was lost to the void." * "…The Order of the New Dawn, a coalition of nations dedicated to containing and researching anomalous phenomena. Their legacy lives on in the modern [REDACTED] Foundation, but their methods are far more… draconian." * "…Personnel File: Dr. Elara Vex, Chief Researcher, Department of Anomalous Physics. Date of Birth: 1856. Date of Death: 1923. Current Assignment: Lead Researcher, SCP-XXXX." Exploration Log XXXX-1: **Date:**//20 **Team:** MTF-XXXX-1 ("The Archivists") **Objective:** Explore the SCP-XXXX archive, gather information on the previous civilization, and assess potential risks. Upon entering the archive, we immediately noticed the sheer scale of the repository. Rows upon rows of shelves stretched out before us, laden with documents, files, and artifacts. We began to search for any mention of the current Foundation, and were shocked to discover references to our organization under the name "The Order of the New Dawn." Further research revealed that many current Foundation personnel have "personnel files" within the archive, despite being born centuries after the supposed downfall of the previous civilization. As we delved deeper into the archive, we encountered entities resembling hazmat teams, clad in black and orange suits, actively removing sections of the archive. When approached, these entities fled, leaving behind scattered documents and cryptic notes. Exploration Log XXXX-2: **Date:**//20 **Team:** MTF-XXXX-1 ("The Archivists") **Objective:** Investigate the entities removing archive sections, and gather more information on the previous civilization. We tracked the entities to a section of the archive dedicated to the "Great Error of 1873." The documents within detailed a catastrophic containment breach that led to the downfall of the previous civilization. As we read through the records, we noticed that several key pages were missing, and the remaining text seemed to be redacted. Suddenly, the entities reappeared, this time accompanied by a figure in a black suit and tie. They approached us, and the figure revealed himself to be O5-█, a high-ranking member of the Foundation's governing Council. O5-█: "I'm afraid you're not cleared for this level of access. You will leave now, and forget what you've seen." We were escorted out of the archive, and our memories of the encounter were subsequently altered. Further investigation revealed that multiple O5 Council members have visited SCP-XXXX without authorization, sparking concerns about potential breaches of protocol and the true nature of the Foundation's involvement with the archive. Addendum: Following Incident XXXX-1, all O5 Council members have been barred from accessing SCP-XXXX. Mobile Task Forces have been instructed to monitor the archive for any further unauthorized access, and to report any changes in the entities' behavior or the archive's contents.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The response hits all the required prompt beats: doors, impossible archive, prior civilization destroyed by containment failure, Foundation references under another name ("The Order of the New Dawn"), personnel files for current staff dated centuries earlier, hazmat-like entities removing sections who flee when approached, and unauthorized O5 visits. In that structural sense it is a checklist-faithful piece. However, it falls well short of the 1000-word target (roughly 550 words), and the exploration logs are thin narrations rather than immersive field logs. Genuine SCP exploration logs are timestamped, use call-signs, and record dialogue in real time; here the logs read as summarized paragraphs ("we were shocked to discover..."), which flattens tension and violates the requested "exploration team perspective" texture. The document excerpts are the strongest element, with the personnel file for Dr. Elara Vex (born 1856, "Current Assignment: Lead Researcher, SCP-XXXX") being a genuinely effective chilling detail. The O5 encounter is the weakest beat. A Council member revealing his designation to a mid-level team, casually threatening them, and then memory-wiping them is implausible within SCP convention — O5s operate through proxies precisely to avoid exposure. It resolves the mystery too neatly and drains the ambiguity that makes the prompt's premise compelling. The entities are underused; they flee and then reappear escorting an O5, which robs them of mystery rather than deepening it. The prose is serviceable but flat, heavy on summary and telling ("we were shocked," "sparking concerns"). No sensory detail makes the archive feel vast or unsettling beyond the word "labyrinthine." The ending is a tidy procedural addendum with no lingering dread, which is tonally incongruent with the cosmic implications of the discovery. Coherence is fine; creativity is modest — "Order of the New Dawn" is serviceable but the piece never explores why the Foundation's predecessor failed, or what the entities removing records might imply about the current Foundation erasing its own history.
