+ Folk horror: Útiseta — Score: 48.7
Prompt:
Fok Horror: Útiseta Write a chapter set in rural northern Sweden in the mid-1990s. Linnea, a teenage girl from Stockholm, has reluctantly moved to a small village with her parents after her father's job transfer. She's befriended Astrid, a local girl who claims her grandmother taught her ancient practices passed down through generations. Astrid has been introducing Linnea to "midnight games" that involve old rituals, convincing her they can use them to learn classmates' secrets or influence their behavior. Tonight, Astrid has convinced Linnea to participate in Útiseta—sitting out on an ancient burial mound at midnight to "receive knowledge from beyond." The chapter focuses on this single midnight excursion, the ritualistic preparations, Astrid's matter-of-fact instructions that mix teenage rebellion with eerily specific traditional elements, and the mounting tension as Linnea becomes increasingly uncertain whether they're simply scaring themselves or if something is actually responding to their presence. The narrative should maintain ambiguity about whether the strange occurrences are supernatural or psychological, while establishing the dangerous line they're crossing by treating ancient practices as entertainment. First person, past tense, told as Linnea's recollection of events in the present day (though this device should be largely transparent). 1000 words. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
I still remember the crunch of gravel beneath my feet as I followed Astrid through the darkness. The trees seemed to loom over us, their branches creaking in the wind like skeletal fingers. It was a sound that would become all too familiar in the months that followed, but on that particular night, it sent shivers down my spine. Astrid had been preparing me for this moment for weeks, ever since she'd introduced me to the concept of Útiseta. "Sitting out" on an ancient burial mound at midnight, she claimed, would allow us to receive knowledge from beyond. I was skeptical, but Astrid's enthusiasm was infectious. She'd shown me the old rituals, the ones her grandmother had taught her, and I'd been drawn in by the sense of rebellion and secrecy. As we walked, the trees grew closer together, casting long, ominous shadows on the ground. Astrid stopped at a massive stone mound, its surface covered in moss and lichen. She turned to me, her eyes gleaming with excitement. "This is it," she whispered. "The burial mound of the old ones. We'll sit here, and they'll share their secrets with us." I felt a shiver run down my spine as I gazed up at the mound. It seemed to loom over us, its presence both ancient and malevolent. Astrid handed me a small pouch containing a few dry herbs and a piece of string. "What's this for?" I asked, my voice barely above a whisper. "We'll use it to call the spirits," Astrid replied, her voice matter-of-fact. "Tie the string around your wrist, and hold the herbs in your hand. It'll help us connect with the other side." I hesitated, feeling a growing sense of unease. This was all starting to feel a bit too real. But Astrid's confidence was reassuring, and I found myself following her instructions. The string felt tight around my wrist, and the herbs smelled earthy and damp. Astrid sat down on the mound, motioning for me to join her. We sat in silence for a few moments, the only sound the wind rustling through the trees. Then, Astrid began to chant, her voice low and hypnotic. I felt a shiver run down my spine as I listened to her words. They sounded like an ancient language, one that I couldn't quite understand. But the rhythm was mesmerizing, and I found myself swaying to the beat. As we sat there, the wind began to pick up, the whispers growing louder. I felt a presence around us, a presence that seemed to be watching us. I glanced at Astrid, but she was lost in her chanting, her eyes closed. Suddenly, a branch snapped in the distance, making me jump. Astrid's eyes flew open, and she grinned at me. "It's working," she whispered. "They're responding to us." I felt a surge of excitement mixed with fear. Was this really happening, or were we just scaring ourselves? I glanced around, but the trees seemed to be closing in on us, their branches tangling together like skeletal fingers. The wind grew stronger, the whispers growing louder. I felt a presence around us, a presence that seemed to be drawing closer. Astrid's chanting grew more intense, her voice rising to a crescendo. And then, everything went silent. The wind died down, the whispers ceased. The trees stood still, their branches motionless. Astrid and I sat there, frozen, waiting for something to happen. But nothing did. The silence was oppressive, heavy with anticipation. I felt like I was holding my breath, waiting for something to break the stillness. And then, a faint whisper seemed to caress my ear. It was a soft, raspy voice, speaking in a language I couldn't understand. I spun around, but there was no one there. Astrid's eyes snapped open, and she grinned at me. "Did you hear that?" she whispered. I nodded, my heart racing. What had we just unleashed? As we sat there, the silence seemed to grow thicker, more oppressive. I felt like we were playing with forces beyond our control, forces that could consume us whole. And then, without warning, Astrid stood up, brushing off her jeans. "Let's get out of here," she said, her voice casual. I followed her, my heart still racing. As we walked away from the mound, I felt a sense of relief wash over me. We'd pushed our luck, and it was time to retreat. But as we walked, the wind began to pick up again, the whispers growing louder. I glanced back at the mound, feeling a sense of unease. Had we really received knowledge from beyond, or had we just scared ourselves silly? I didn't know, but I knew one thing for sure – I'd never look at those trees, or that mound, in the same way again. As we walked back to the village, the darkness seemed to press in around us, the shadows growing longer and more sinister. I felt like we'd crossed a line, a line that we couldn't uncross. And I knew that our midnight games were only just beginning.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece hits the basic beats of the prompt—two girls, a mound, a ritual, ambiguity—but does so in a generic, formulaic way. The prose is repetitive ("I felt a shiver run down my spine" appears twice; "skeletal fingers" twice; "presence around us" twice), and the tension relies on clichés (looming trees, whispers, sudden silence). The 1990s rural Sweden setting is entirely absent—no period detail, no village texture, no sense of place beyond generic dark woods. Astrid's "matter-of-fact instructions mixing teenage rebellion with eerily specific traditional elements" is underdeveloped; the ritual details (string, herbs, chanting) are vague rather than specific. The first-person retrospective framing is stated but barely used. Dialogue is thin and functional. The ending ("our midnight games were only just beginning") is a stock cliffhanger. Ambiguity is maintained, but through vagueness rather than craft. Word count is roughly on target. Coherent and readable, but flat and clichéd. + Physical-spatial: Therapeutic Boundaries — Score: 54.5
Prompt:
**"Therapeutic Boundaries"** Write a first-person narrative from the perspective of a former professional athlete undergoing mandatory physical therapy after a career-destroying injury, harboring resentment toward recovery itself. Their physiotherapist is not only unnervingly attractive but also a former training partner from their competitive days who has successfully transitioned to a new career while the protagonist's identity remains shattered. We'll lean into the sexual tension of the scene by having her be attracted to him, but both of them attempting to maintain mental distance while being in physical contact as he shows her stretches & movements & manipulates her body, navigating the thresholds of pain. This scene MUST maintain complete physical coherence—every movement, stretch, and manipulation must be anatomically precise and spatially consistent. Paint an exceptionally vivid sensory picture through detailed attention to pain thresholds, involuntary responses to touch. Capture the suppressed, flaring tension of physical proximity, touch, and the controlled infliction of uncomfortable movements. Physical clarity is essential — readers must understand exactly what is happening with the body during each therapeutic technique. 1000 words.
Model Output:
I lay on the therapy table, my eyes fixed on the ceiling as Dr. Lee's hands moved with a practiced gentleness over my injured leg. Her fingers danced across the scar tissue, palpating the knots and adhesions that had formed after years of pushing my body to its limits. I winced, a reflexive jerk of pain that she acknowledged with a soft "easy" as she eased off the pressure. We'd known each other back in the day, when we were both competing at the top of our game. I'd been the star athlete, the one with the endorsement deals and the championship titles. She'd been the up-and-comer, the talented young gun who always seemed to be one step behind me. But now, she was the one with the steady career and the thriving practice, while I was the broken, battered shell of a man clinging to the memories of past glories. Her touch was like a spark to dry kindling, igniting a firestorm of conflicting emotions within me. I resented her for being whole and healthy, for having moved on from our shared past with such apparent ease. I resented her for being so damned attractive, with her bright smile and her colorful compression sleeves – a relic from our training days, a reminder of what we'd once shared. And I resented her for being the one to witness my vulnerability, to see me at my weakest and most exposed. As she worked, her fingers tracing the contours of my leg, I felt my muscles tense and relax in a rhythm that was almost... intimate. It was as if my body was responding to her touch on a primal level, despite my brain's best efforts to shut down the reaction. My gaze drifted down to where her hands were working, and I caught a glimpse of the patterns on her sleeves – a riot of color and texture that seemed to pulse with a life of their own. "Okay, let's try some passive range of motion," she said, her voice low and smooth as she grasped my ankle and began to gently rotate my foot. "I want you to relax and let me do the work. Feel the movement, but don't try to control it." I gritted my teeth as she flexed my foot up and back, the motion sending a ripple of pain through my calf and knee. My leg twitched, trying to jerk away from the discomfort, but she held firm, her hands a steady presence that kept me grounded. As she worked, her sleeves rode up her arms, revealing a glimpse of toned muscle beneath. I felt a flicker of attraction, quickly suppressed as I reminded myself that this was therapy, not a social engagement. But it was hard to shake the feeling that we were dancing around something more, that the tension between us was building with every passing moment. She moved on to my hip, using her body weight to apply gentle traction as she rocked me back and forth. I felt my pelvis tilt, my lower back releasing a knot of tension that had been building for weeks. It was a sensation that was both pleasurable and painful, like the gentle scratching of an itch that had been driving me mad. As she worked, her face was a mask of concentration, her eyes narrowed in focus. But I caught glimpses of something more – a spark of warmth, a flicker of recognition that seemed to acknowledge the unspoken tension between us. It was as if she knew exactly what I was feeling, and was working to keep her own emotions in check. The session drew to a close, and she helped me sit up, her hands at my elbows as she guided me through a series of gentle stretches. I felt my muscles lengthen, my joints releasing a series of satisfying cracks and pops as she coaxed me through the movements. As I stood, testing my weight on my injured leg, I caught her eye and felt a jolt of electricity run through me. It was a moment of pure connection, a spark of attraction that seemed to hang in the air like a challenge. But I knew better than to act on it. This was therapy, not a date. And I was a patient, not a partner. "Thanks, Doc," I said, my voice a little rougher than I'd intended. She smiled, her eyes crinkling at the corners. "Anytime, champ. Now let's get you scheduled for next week's appointment." As I limped out of the office, I couldn't shake the feeling that I was walking into a minefield, one that was fraught with danger and uncertainty. But I was ready to take the risk, to see where this fragile, fraught connection might lead.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The response delivers a competent but generic therapy scene. It does maintain physical coherence reasonably well—passive range of motion, ankle rotation, hip traction with body weight—and the pain/pleasure thresholds are described with some clarity. However, several weaknesses stand out. The prose leans heavily on cliché: "spark to dry kindling," "jolt of electricity," "a riot of color and texture," "mask of concentration." The emotional tension is repeatedly named rather than dramatized ("I resented her for...," "I felt a flicker of attraction, quickly suppressed"). The backstory is delivered as flat exposition rather than emerging through scene. The word count falls short of the 1000-word seed at roughly 750 words. The prompt's specific dynamic—both characters actively suppressing attraction while navigating physical contact—is only lightly sketched. The therapist's perspective is entirely inferred; her "flicker of recognition" is asserted, not shown. The competitive history ("one step behind me") is a promising seed but never becomes specific or textured—no named sport, no concrete shared memory. The ending is a mild problem: the final paragraph pivots to optimism ("I was ready to take the risk") without earning it. The session itself showed resentment and suppression, not a shift toward willingness. The "minefield" metaphor is mixed and vague. Dialogue is serviceable but thin ("Thanks, Doc" / "Anytime, champ")—the "champ" beat is one of the few effective character touches. The piece is coherent and readable, never confusing, but it plays every beat safely and predictably. + Historical fiction: High Seas and Low Vices — Score: 53.0
Prompt:
High Seas and Low Vices: British Naval Officer in the Opium Trade Character: Lieutenant James Merrick, a high-ranking officer in the British Royal Navy stationed in Canton, China in the 1830s. Once promising and decorated, now struggling with a severe opium addiction he hides from his superiors. Synopsis: Write a chapter following Lieutenant Merrick during a single day as he shirks his official naval duties in what has become a daily need to satiate the dragon. Told primarily in dialogue with the characters he interacts with. Focus on brief slices of his journey through the shadowy underworld of Canton: interactions with acquaintances, negotiations with local dealers, schemes and unsavoury jobs taken to fund his habit. Writing Task: Use rich sensory details to describe a day in the life of James Merrick. Write for a real sense of time & place while avoiding cliche. First person, past tense. 1000 words.
Model Output:
I woke to the sound of my own ragged breathing, the stench of last night's opium clinging to my skin like a shroud. The sun had barely cracked the horizon, casting a pale glow over the cramped, dingy room I called home in the British concession of Canton. I lay there for a moment, feeling the weight of my own guilt settle upon me like a physical force. A harsh, coppery taste lingered at the back of my throat, and I let out a cough that felt like it was tearing my lungs apart. I covered my mouth with a shaking hand, the familiar ache spreading through my chest. "Damn this sea air," I muttered to myself, a lie I'd been telling others – and myself – for months now. I threw off the covers and swung my legs over the side of the bed, my eyes scanning the room for the familiar shape of my pipe. It was nowhere to be seen, and a cold dread began to seep into my bones. I stumbled out of bed, my legs trembling beneath me, and staggered over to the small dresser against the wall. The pipe lay on top, next to a small pouch of opium and a few scattered coins. I let out a shaky breath and poured a small amount of the precious powder into the pipe, my hands moving with a practiced ease. The first hit was like a warm hug, spreading through my veins and easing the tight, anxious feeling that had been building in my chest. I took another hit, feeling my eyelids begin to droop, and then another, until the room was spinning and I was floating on a cloud of sweet, sweet nothingness. The sound of knocking at the door brought me back to reality. I groggily opened my eyes to find my servant, Ah-Kong, standing in the doorway with a look of concern etched on his face. "Sir, you are late for your duties," he said, his voice low and urgent. "The captain will be expecting you on the quarterdeck within the hour." I waved a hand dismissively, feeling a surge of irritation. "Tell him I'm...indisposed, Ah-Kong. I'll be there as soon as I can." Ah-Kong's eyes narrowed, but he nodded and backed out of the room, closing the door behind him. I knew he suspected something, but he was too loyal to say anything. Yet. I lay back on the bed, feeling the opium begin to wear off, and knew I had to get moving. I couldn't afford to be late again, not with Captain Fanshawe breathing down my neck. But first, I needed to replenish my supply. I threw on some clothes and made my way out into the crowded streets of Canton, dodging coolies and merchants as I made my way to the old warehouse district. The smell of saltwater and coal smoke hung heavy in the air, and I could hear the sound of gulls crying overhead. I spotted my contact, a burly Chinese man named Wong, standing outside a dilapidated warehouse on the edge of the district. He nodded at me as I approached, his eyes flicking back and forth with a mixture of curiosity and wariness. "Lieutenant Merrick," he said, his voice low and gravelly. "What can I do for you?" "I need more opium, Wong," I said, trying to keep my voice steady. "And I need it now." Wong's expression turned calculating, and he rubbed his chin thoughtfully. "I might have some...available, Lieutenant. But it's going to cost you. A lot." I hesitated, feeling a cold sweat break out on my forehead. I knew I couldn't afford it, not on my naval salary. But I had to have it. "I'll do a job for you, Wong," I said finally, the words tasting like ash in my mouth. "Whatever you need, I'll do it. Just give me the opium." Wong's eyes gleamed with a mixture of curiosity and contempt, and he nodded slowly. "I think I might have something that requires...your particular set of skills, Lieutenant. Meet me at the old windmill on the outskirts of town at midnight. And come alone." I nodded, feeling a sense of trepidation building in my chest. I knew I was in deep, and I had no idea how I was going to get out. The rest of the day passed in a blur of naval duties and half-hearted attempts to focus. I went through the motions, but my mind was elsewhere, consumed by the constant, gnawing need for opium. As the sun began to set over the city, I made my way back to my quarters, feeling like I was walking through a nightmare. Ah-Kong was waiting for me, his expression stern. "Sir, the captain wants to see you in his quarters. Now." I nodded, feeling a sense of dread building in my chest. I knew I was in trouble, and I had no idea how I was going to get out of it. As I made my way to the captain's quarters, I couldn't shake the feeling that I was trapped in a living hell of my own making, with no escape in sight.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece is serviceable but generic. It fulfills the basic brief—first person, past tense, a day in the life of an addicted naval officer in Canton, with dialogue-driven scenes—but does so with little distinction. Strengths: The structure is coherent and follows the prompt's arc (waking, scoring opium, shirking duties, a dealer negotiation, a midnight job offer, a cliffhanger with the captain). The prose is readable and the pacing moves briskly. The premise itself has inherent tension. Weaknesses: The sensory detail is thin and clichéd ("sweet, sweet nothingness," "walking through a nightmare," "trapped in a living hell of my own making"). Canton in the 1830s is barely rendered—no specific streets, sounds, foods, dialects, or textures of the Thirteen Factories; the setting could be any port. The dialogue is flat and functional, with Wong speaking like a stock villain ("come alone," "your particular set of skills"). Ah-Kong is a stereotype of the loyal servant. The narrative repeatedly tells us what Merrick feels ("feeling the weight of my own guilt," "a sense of dread building in my chest") rather than dramatizing it. The opium experience is described in vague, secondhand terms rather than the specific phenomenology of the drug. The midnight windmill meeting is an unearned hook—windmills on the outskirts of Canton are geographically incongruous. The ending is a shrug rather than a resolution, though the prompt asked for a single day, so this is partially forgivable. The prose leans on em-dashes and formulaic sentence rhythms ("I knew X, and I had no idea how I was going to get out of it" appears twice nearly verbatim, suggesting rushed drafting). The piece is competent fanfare-level writing: clear, coherent, but without a single surprising image, line, or character beat. + Humour: Sisyphus Gives Advice — Score: 50.2
Prompt:
Sisyphus Gives Advice Write a comedic scene in the style of Terry Pratchett featuring Sisyphus, who has started an advice column while continuing his eternal boulder-pushing. Rather than being bitter about his punishment, Sisyphus has developed a comfortably zen emotional intelligence and wisdom from his centuries of repetitive labor. Not in the cliche ways you might expect -- he's actually very grounded, warm & connected with the everyday human plight. He dictates responses to "Crow," his feathered familiar who serves as both scribe and mail carrier. The piece should include Sisyphus thoughtfully answering several advice-seekers' letters about everyday problems (relationship troubles, work-life balance, dealing with difficult people, life purpose doubts) while periodically returning to his boulder-pushing duties. His advice should be surprisingly insightful and practical. The humor should emerge naturally from the messages he receives, from mundane to risque. Sisyphus should genuinely enjoy helping others, finding meaning in this new pursuit while maintaining his boulder routine. The humour should be *understated and not forced*. Contrary to expectation, almost none of his advice involves boulder rolling metaphors (though he does sneak *one* subtle in there), nor a treatise on stoicism. We are also going against the obvious fish-out-of-water trope; in fact, he's surprisingly a natural at this. In all of this , it's important to *show, don't tell*. The format should be largely epistolary, with the occasional interjection for scene setting. 1000 words.
Model Output:
The sun rose over the dusty, rocky terrain as Sisyphus leaned against the massive boulder, wiping the sweat from his brow. Crow, perched on a nearby rock, scribbled on a piece of parchment with a quill. "Ready for today's batch, Crow?" Sisyphus asked, his voice low and soothing. Crow cawed and nodded, adjusting his spectacles. "Always ready, Sisyphus. What's the first letter?" Sisyphus cleared his throat and began to dictate. "Dear Loopy in Lycourgos, I'm having trouble with my partner. We've been together for a few years, and it feels like we're just going through the motions. We don't fight, but we don't really talk either. I feel like I'm just pretending to be in love. What should I do? Yours sincerely, Lost in Lycourgos" Sisyphus paused, considering the boulder as he pondered the response. He pushed it a few feet up the hill, the familiar rumble a soothing accompaniment to his thoughts. "Dear Lost in Lycourgos, I'm not going to tell you to 'roll with it' or 'push through the tough times.' What I will say is that love is a lot like... well, not exactly like pushing a boulder up a hill, but it does require effort. Not the grand, sweeping gestures, but the small, everyday ones. Try having a conversation with your partner about something that's not just 'what's for dinner?' or 'whose turn is it to do the dishes?' Find something you both love, or used to love, and do it together. See if that sparks something. And if not, well, it's okay to acknowledge that sometimes people grow apart. It's not a failure, just a new path. Wishing you clarity, Sisyphus" Crow scribbled furiously as Sisyphus continued. "Next letter, please!" A minor deity, Bureaucratus, appeared beside Sisyphus, adjusting his toga. "What's all this I hear about you giving advice, Sisyphus? I mean, really, you're a mythological figure, not a... a... an agony aunt!" Sisyphus chuckled and continued pushing the boulder. "Ah, Bureaucratus, you know as well as I do that I've had a lot of time on my hands. I figure I might as well use it to help others. Besides, it's not about being an 'agony aunt'; it's about sharing what I've learned from my... unique perspective." Bureaucratus snorted. "Unique perspective? You mean being stuck in an endless cycle of drudgery? Ha! You're not exactly qualified to give advice on happiness and fulfillment!" Sisyphus smiled, his eyes crinkling at the corners. "Actually, I think that's exactly what qualifies me. I've had centuries to reflect on what truly matters. And I've found that it's not the grand gestures or the epic quests that bring happiness, but the small, everyday moments of connection and kindness." Crow looked up from his scribbling. "Next letter, Sisyphus?" "Ah, yes! The next one, please." "Dear Sisyphus, I'm having trouble finding meaning in my life. I feel like I'm just going through the motions, working a job I don't love, and wondering if this is all there is. I've tried hobbies and travel, but nothing seems to stick. What's the point of it all? Sincerely, Lost in Limbo" Sisyphus nodded thoughtfully, pushing the boulder a bit further up the hill. "Dear Lost in Limbo, I'm not going to tell you to 'find your passion' or 'follow your dreams.' What I will say is that meaning is often found in the mundane. Not in some grand, earth-shattering epiphany, but in the small, everyday moments of connection with others and with the world around you. Try volunteering, or helping a friend with a project. Sometimes, it's not about finding the 'meaning of life,' but about finding meaning in the life you already have. Wishing you clarity, Sisyphus" As Sisyphus finished dictating, a letter arrived via a messenger bird. Crow took it from the bird's beak and read the contents, raising an eyebrow. "Sisyphus, this one's a bit... unusual. It's from someone asking for advice on how to deal with their significant other's 'excessive flatulence.'" Sisyphus chuckled. "Ah, now that's a challenge. Let's see... " He leaned against the boulder, thinking for a moment before dictating his response. "Dear Gassy in Gaul, I think the key here is to approach the situation with empathy and understanding. It's not about 'fixing' your partner's flatulence, but about finding ways to... ah, how can I put this delicately... 'minimize the impact.' Consider a gentle conversation about the issue, and maybe invest in some air fresheners. And remember, relationships are about compromise and understanding. If you can't laugh about it, at least try to find the humor in the situation. Wishing you a fresher relationship, Sisyphus" As the sun began to set, casting a warm orange glow over the landscape, Sisyphus smiled, feeling a sense of contentment wash over him. "You know, Crow, I think I've found a new purpose. One that doesn't involve just pushing a boulder up a hill." Crow looked up from his scribbling, a twinkle in his eye. "I think you're quite good at this, Sisyphus. You're a natural." Sisyphus chuckled, pushing the boulder a bit further up the hill. "I think it's just a matter of perspective, my fine feathered friend. Sometimes, it's the smallest, most mundane moments that bring the greatest joy."
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece is serviceable but flat. Its central failing is that it ignores several explicit prompt constraints. The prompt asked for almost no boulder metaphors (one subtle one allowed); the response leans on boulder comparisons repeatedly ("love is a lot like... not exactly like pushing a boulder up a hill, but it does require effort"). It also explicitly tells rather than shows: Sisyphus announces "I think I've found a new purpose" and Crow states "You're a natural," which the prompt asked to be demonstrated, not declared. The Bureaucratus interlude is precisely the skeptical-foil trope the prompt discouraged, and his challenge is resolved with a tidy, unearned affirmation. The advice itself is generic self-help ("meaning is often found in the mundane," "try volunteering") rather than the "surprisingly insightful and practical" wisdom requested. The humor is mild and occasionally forced (the flatulence letter is the strongest beat, but its answer is toothless). The Pratchett style is absent — there's no footnoted asides, no absurdist precision, no satirical bite; the prose is plain, workmanlike American workshop prose with Greek set dressing. The "Lost in Lycourgos"/"Lost in Limbo" naming is a nice touch, and the epistolary structure is competently handled. The ending is neat and warm but predictable, and the emotional register never varies — Sisyphus is serene from line one, so there's no arc, no texture, no complexity. Dialogue is weak: Crow is a cipher, Bureaucratus is a strawman, and Sisyphus speaks in aphorisms. Descriptions are minimal and clichéd ("sun rose," "warm orange glow"). Coherence is fine; the piece never confuses. But it's safe, unsurprising, and reads like a first draft that satisfied the bullet points of the prompt without inhabiting its spirit. + Coming of age: Lost & Found in Osaka — Score: 41.3
Prompt:
Lost & Found in Osaka Slice of Life / Coming-of-Age: First Connection. Write one scene within a larger story set in Osaka, Japan. A shy, introverted teenager & synthesizer nerd, is browsing the electronics district of Den Den Town. She is drawn to the sound of an electric guitar. A girl, slightly older and radiating confidence, is busking near the entrance to a crowded arcade, playing a blistering rendition of a classic anime theme. The scene takes us through their initial awkward interaction, transitioning into a surprisingly easy conversation. The busker, intrigued by the teenager's quiet intensity and knowledge of music, suggests they grab some sushi at a nearby kaitenzushi (conveyor belt sushi) restaurant. Write primarily in dialogue, capturing the distinct voices of the two characters: the teenager's hesitant and -- initially -- awkward speech, and the busker's more outgoing and playful tone. 1000 words.
Model Output:
**Scene: Den Den Town, Osaka** The teenager, Mei, wandered down the crowded street, her eyes scanning the rows of electronics shops and arcades. She had spent the morning browsing the synthesizer stores, trying out different models and imagining the sounds she could create. As she approached the entrance to a popular arcade, the sound of an electric guitar caught her attention. A girl with short, spiky hair and a bright smile was playing a blistering rendition of the "Attack on Titan" theme song. Mei's eyes widened as she stopped to listen. The busker, whose name was Akira, finished her song to a smattering of applause and coins tossed into her guitar case. She grinned at Mei, who quickly looked away, her cheeks flushing. Akira: (laughing) Ah, thanks for stopping to listen! You're a fan of Levi's theme, huh? Mei: (nervously) Y-yes... I love the anime... Akira: (teasingly) Oh, a fellow fan! What's your favorite part of the series? Mei: (hesitantly) I-I like the music... the soundtrack is really good... Akira: (impressed) No way! I'm a huge fan of Hiroyuki Sawano's work. Have you listened to his other soundtracks? Mei: (surprised) Y-yes... I love his work on "Aldnoah.Zero"... Akira: (excitedly) Ah, me too! That soundtrack is insane! (pauses, studying Mei) You know, you seem like someone who'd appreciate a good conversation about music. Want to grab some sushi with me? Mei: (taken aback) S-sushi? I... I don't know... Akira: (persistent) Come on, it'll be fun! I know this great kaitenzushi place just around the corner. We can talk about music and anime and all that nerdy stuff. (smiling) I promise I won't bite... unless you're a fan of Kpop, then we might have to fight. Mei: (laughing nervously) I-I'm not a fan of Kpop... Akira: (grinning) Excellent! Follow me! Akira packed up her guitar and led Mei to a small kaitenzushi restaurant nearby. They slid into a booth and began selecting plates from the conveyor belt. Akira: (piling her plate high with sushi) So, what do you think of the new season of anime? Anything catching your eye? Mei: (hesitantly) I-I've been watching "Made in Abyss"... it's really good... Akira: (impressed) Ah, nice! The music in that show is amazing. Have you listened to the soundtrack? Mei: (smiling) Y-yes... it's really beautiful... Akira: (excitedly) Right?! I know, I've been listening to it nonstop. (pauses, studying Mei) You know, I've been playing music for years, but I've never met anyone who shares my passion for anime soundtracks. What do you like about them? Mei: (opening up) I-I love how they can evoke emotions and create atmosphere... it's like they're a character in the story... Akira: (smiling) Exactly! Music can make or break a scene. I love how you think. (pauses, taking a bite of sushi) You know, I've been thinking of starting a music project, maybe a band or something. Would you be interested in collaborating? Mei: (surprised) M-me? I don't know... I'm not very good at playing instruments... Akira: (encouragingly) Nonsense! You have a great ear for music. I can tell. Besides, I'm not looking for perfection – I want someone who shares my passion and is willing to experiment. (smiling) Who knows, maybe we can create something amazing together. Mei: (smiling back) I-I'd like that... As they finished their sushi and parted ways, Mei felt a sense of excitement and possibility. She had never felt such an easy connection with someone before, especially not someone as outgoing and confident as Akira. As they exchanged numbers and made plans to meet up again, Mei couldn't help but wonder what other adventures lay ahead. Akira: (smiling) Hey, thanks for the chat, Mei. I had a blast. Let's do it again soon? Mei: (smiling back) Y-yes... I'd like that... As Mei walked away, she felt a sense of belonging she had never experienced before. She had found a kindred spirit in Akira, and she couldn't wait to see what the future held for them.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The response hits the basic plot beats requested (Den Den Town, busker, anime theme, sushi invitation) but executes them in a flat, mechanical way. The dialogue is written in script format with parenthetical stage directions ("(nervously)", "(excitedly)") rather than rendered as prose — a major amateurish choice that undercuts the "write primarily in dialogue" instruction, which clearly intended dialogue within narrative prose. The parentheticals also do the emotional work the dialogue itself should do, a textbook case of tell-don't-show. The conversation is surprisingly generic: two characters agreeing that soundtracks are good, naming real anime, and bonding instantly over shared tastes. There's no friction, no distinct voice beyond Mei's stammering ("Y-yes...") and Akira's exclamation points. Akira's "playful" lines are mostly filler ("I promise I won't bite... unless you're a fan of Kpop, then we might have to fight" — a nonsensical joke). Mei's "quiet intensity and knowledge of music" from the prompt is asserted by Akira ("You have a great ear for music. I can tell.") rather than demonstrated — the reader never sees Mei do anything musically knowledgeable beyond naming a soundtrack. The connection between the two is unearned: within minutes Akira invites Mei to sushi, then proposes a band collaboration, and Mei — described as shy and introverted — agrees to everything with almost no resistance. The ending piles on explicit narration of Mei's feelings ("a sense of belonging she had never experienced before") rather than letting the scene carry it. The final paragraphs repeat the same sentiment twice, and the closing exchange ("Let's do it again soon?" / "Y-yes... I'd like that...") duplicates an earlier near-identical exchange. Imagery is nearly absent — Den Den Town, the arcade, and the kaitenzushi restaurant are barely sketched. Prose is serviceable but bland, with no sensory texture of Osaka. The piece is coherent and complete, and the character dynamic is at least legible, but it reads like a competent outline rather than a lived-in scene. + Teen horror: Tick Tock (Goosebumps) — Score: 48.2
Prompt:
Teen Horror (Goosebumps style) Write a chapter where a middle school student discovers their new smart watch has the ability to rewind time, but doesn't realize that each use is aging them until they notice physical changes (like accelerating through puberty) and that people are treating them differently. They inadvertently cause a timeline shift with disastrous consequences -- and it can only be rectified (maybe?) by reversing the clock once more. Length: 1000 words.
Model Output:
Chapter 1: The Timekeeper I ripped open the packaging on my birthday present, a sleek black smartwatch with a touchscreen face and a built-in fitness tracker. My parents had gotten it for me, saying it would help me stay organized and on top of my schedule. I was skeptical, but the watch looked cool, so I strapped it on and began to explore its features. As I scrolled through the settings, I stumbled upon an unusual icon – a clock with an arrow pointing counterclockwise. I tapped it, and the watch face flickered to show a countdown: 00:00:10. Suddenly, the room around me began to rewind like a video on fast forward. I stumbled backward, watching in amazement as my parents rewrapped the gift and handed it to me once again. "Whoa, what the...?" I stuttered. My mom laughed. "Happy birthday, kiddo! Isn't it great?" I stared at the watch, my mind reeling. This thing could rewind time? I felt like I was in a science fiction movie. Over the next few days, I experimented with the watch, rewinding time to relive moments I wanted to redo. I aced a test I'd initially failed, turned a bad joke into a funny one, and even avoided getting picked last in gym class. It was exhilarating, like having a superpower. But as the days passed, I started to notice strange changes. My voice was getting deeper, my hair was growing faster, and I was sprouting acne like crazy. At first, I chalked it up to puberty, but it was happening at an alarming rate. I looked in the mirror one morning, and my eyes widened in shock. My face was changing, my features shifting from soft and rounded to sharp and angular. People at school were treating me differently too. The popular kids, who had always ignored me, were suddenly flirting with me in the hallways. Teachers were giving me more attention, praising me for my "maturity." I felt like I was living in a dream, but it was freaking me out. That's when I realized the truth: each time I rewound the clock, I was aging. The watch was accelerating my growth, and I had no idea how to stop it. Panic set in as I stared at the countdown on the watch face: 00:05:00. I had to be careful, but I couldn't resist the temptation to use the watch one more time. I rewound a conversation with my best friend, Emily, to avoid an awkward argument. But when I re-emerged into the present, something was off. The school hallways were empty, and the classrooms were dark. A sign on the principal's office door read: "School Closed – Due to Unforeseen Circumstances." I stumbled through the deserted hallways, searching for answers. That's when I saw the newspaper on the bulletin board: "Local School Destroyed in Mysterious Fire." My heart sank as I read the date – it was tomorrow's news. I had caused a timeline shift. The watch face flickered again, displaying the countdown: 00:02:00. I knew I had to reverse the clock once more, but what if it made things worse? What if I lost more time, or altered the course of events in unpredictable ways? I took a deep breath and tapped the icon. The world around me rewound once more, and I closed my eyes, hoping against hope that I could fix the timeline and turn back the clock on my own accelerated aging. As the countdown reached zero, I opened my eyes to find myself back in the present, the school bustling with students. I exhaled slowly, relieved. But as I looked down at the watch, I saw the countdown had reset: 00:10:00. I knew I had to be more careful. The watch was a double-edged sword, granting me incredible power but also exacting a steep price. I vowed to use it sparingly, if at all, and to find a way to reverse the aging process before it was too late. But as I walked through the crowded hallways, I couldn't shake the feeling that I was running out of time.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece hits the basic plot beats requested: smartwatch discovery, time rewind, accelerated aging, social treatment changes, timeline disaster, and a final rewind attempt. However, execution is flat and rushed. The prose is plain and summary-driven ("Over the next few days, I experimented with the watch..."), relying heavily on telling rather than showing. The aging conceit is underdeveloped — the narrator notices changes in a few generic sentences, and the "people treat me differently" element is conveyed in one paragraph of exposition rather than dramatized scenes. The timeline shift (school destroyed in a fire) appears and is resolved within a few paragraphs with no real stakes, tension, or explanation — the final rewind simply works, deflating the "maybe?" ambiguity the prompt called for. The countdown mechanic (00:10:00, 00:05:00, 00:02:00) is introduced without clear rules; it seems to tick down per use but its meaning is never established, making it feel arbitrary. Dialogue is minimal and generic ("Whoa, what the...?"). The ending is tidy and low-stakes, with a mild hook ("running out of time") that doesn't earn much dread. Characterization is thin — the narrator has no distinct voice, and Emily exists only as a name. The twist that each rewind ages the narrator is stated outright rather than discovered through escalating dread. It reads like a competent but perfunctory outline fleshed out into prose, roughly at the requested length, but lacking the Goosebumps flavor of playful menace and cliffhanger tension. + Noir romance: Carnival of Dangerous Intentions — Score: 53.8
Prompt:
Carnival of Dangerous Intentions Coney Island, New York, July 1937. She stopped just before reaching the shooting gallery set up along the boardwalk. It was near dusk, and the carnival lights were buzzing on. **His locks**. The words flashed through her mind, again, like an intruder. Such an old-fashioned turn of phrase that kept surfacing, and something else -- dangerously compelling -- quickly suppressed, lest she question why she had returned here at all. This place where she had promised to meet the stranger -- the young man with the obsidian curls and even darker eyes. She didn't even know his name. Write a scene in a larger story inspired by this prompt. The scene should capture an atmosphere that blends romantic allure with an undercurrent of subtle danger or moral ambiguity. Think 50 shades, but with a more restrained classic sensibility. The scene is all about establishing tension. Write for the feminine perspective. Include roughly 50% dialogue, 20% internal monologue, and 30% exposition and scene setting. The tone should have some edge to it. 1000 words.
Model Output:
As she stood before the shooting gallery, the sound of popping balloons and the murmur of the crowd created a sense of disorientation. The smell of cotton candy wafted through the air, transporting her to a childhood memory that lingered just out of reach. She couldn't quite place it, but the sweetness brought a pang of nostalgia. Her gaze drifted to the young man with the obsidian curls, standing by the gallery's entrance. His eyes, like two dark pools, seemed to draw her in. He was even more striking than she remembered, and a shiver ran down her spine as their eyes met. She felt a sudden jolt of uncertainty, wondering if she had made a mistake by agreeing to meet him here. As she approached, he smiled, and his eyes crinkled at the corners. "You're right on time," he said, his voice low and smooth. "I was starting to think you'd changed your mind." She shook her head, feeling a flutter in her chest. "I wouldn't have missed it for the world," she replied, trying to sound nonchalant despite the butterflies in her stomach. He chuckled and offered his arm, leading her into the gallery. The sound of laughter and music enveloped them, and the air was thick with the smell of gunpowder and sugar. They walked past the shooting stalls, where men were trying their luck at winning stuffed animals for their sweethearts. As they strolled, he asked her about her interests, and she found herself opening up to him in ways she hadn't expected. He listened intently, his eyes never leaving hers, and she felt a sense of connection she couldn't quite explain. But beneath the surface, she sensed a current of tension. It was as if he was hiding something, and she couldn't quite put her finger on what it was. She pushed the thought aside, telling herself she was just being paranoid. They stopped at a stall selling balloon darts, and he picked up a balloon, his fingers brushing against hers as he handed it to her. "Try your luck," he said, his eyes glinting with amusement. As she took aim, he stood close behind her, his breath whispering against her ear. "You're not very good at this, are you?" he teased, his voice sending shivers down her spine. She laughed, feeling a sense of playfulness she hadn't experienced in a long time. "I guess not," she admitted, trying to focus on the target. He reached around her, his arm brushing against hers, and adjusted her aim. "Let me help you," he said, his voice low and husky. As they stood there, the crowd around them melted away, and all she could feel was the warmth of his body against hers. She felt a spark of attraction, and her heart began to pound in her chest. Suddenly, the balloon popped, and she jumped, laughing. He chuckled, his eyes crinkling at the corners, and handed her the prize – a stuffed rabbit with a wonky eye. As they walked away from the stall, she felt a sense of unease creeping in. What was she doing here, with a stranger, in a place that felt increasingly surreal? The smell of cotton candy still lingered in the air, but it no longer brought her joy. "Tell me," she said, her voice barely above a whisper, "what's your name?" As they walked further into the carnival, the lights and sounds blurring around them, she felt herself being drawn into a world that was both exhilarating and terrifying. She knew she should be careful, but she couldn't help the sense of excitement that was building inside her. The words "his locks" still lingered in her mind, a reminder of the danger that lurked beneath the surface. But she pushed the thought aside, letting herself get swept up in the moment. As they disappeared into the crowd, the sound of laughter and music fading into the distance, she felt a sense of trepidation. What had she gotten herself into? And what secrets lay hidden behind those dark, piercing eyes?

+ Surreal horror: Caribou Season at the Lodge — Score: 50.2
Prompt:
Caribou Season at the Lodge Genre: Surreal Horror JT knew something wasn't right from the moment they'd pulled up to the lodge. It looked exactly as it had every autumn of his life—the cedar shingles silvered with age, the porch sagging gently under weight of winters past—but the air felt thicker, somehow, as if the woods had leaned in closer, waiting. He thought maybe he was just tired. Maybe it was nothing more than the long drive north, cramped in the back of the truck beside his older brother Mike -- and a stack of rifles wrapped in oily canvas. Still, the feeling lingered. The first caribou they found that season hadn't bolted. It had just watched, ears flicking lazily, waiting for the shot. JT had felt a crawling in his gut watching it die, noticing how it fell with a quiet dignity—as though it had wanted nothing more. Nobody else seemed to find it odd, or at least they didn't say so. JT kept his mouth shut. Always safer that way. Then there was the moment in the clearing. JT had fallen behind, fumbling with bootlaces stiff from the cold, and by the time he'd caught up with his father and brother, they'd already stopped. JT slowed instinctively, ducking behind a tangle of brush, staring out into the open space where a massive buck stood utterly still, chest rising and falling in slow, deep breaths. The animal looked…wrong, somehow. Maybe it was the eyes—dark and flat, calm in a way that made JT's neck prickle. Or maybe it was something deeper, something in the way the buck stood close enough for his father to reach out and touch it. JT watched as his father raised a trembling hand toward the animal's muzzle. His father's face had a strange expression, eyes too bright, lips parted slightly, like he was on the verge of whispering something—a secret, maybe. The rifle hung uselessly at his side, forgotten. Mike had the same dazed look in his eyes. JT's breath stopped short, his mind suddenly shouting at him to look away, turn around, just run—but he couldn't move. And then, as suddenly as it had begun, it was over. The buck shivered, startled by something unseen, and disappeared into the brush without so much as a sound. The two of them looked around, confused, seemingly surprised to find themselves where they stood. JT stayed hidden, breathing shallowly, fear mingling with a confused sense of shame. His father had failed to take the shot. To even think about mentioning what he'd seen would have felt like betrayal—or something worse. JT waited until the others moved on before stepping out from cover. He stared into the empty clearing for a long, uneasy moment, wondering if anything had really happened at all, or if he was losing his grip. Maybe the woods were finally getting into his head. Writing Instructions: Continue with the next chapter of the story, maintaining a style reminiscent of Stephen King—grounded realism blended seamlessly with unsettling surrealism. The atmosphere should convey subtle dread, isolation, and psychological ambiguity. JT's perspective is crucial, yet he is an unreliable narrator, caught in uncertainty about what he's perceiving. Establish an undertone of creeping dread, establishing the isolation of JT's perspective, which he largely keeps to himself. Incorporate some dialogue. Build tension towards a payoff. 1000 words.
Model Output:
Chapter 2: The Guide's Warning The rest of the day's hunt was a blur for JT. He went through the motions, but his mind kept drifting back to the clearing. What had just happened? Had he really seen what he thought he saw? The image of his father's trembling hand, the buck's unblinking gaze, and the eerie silence that had followed lingered in his mind like a bad omen. As the sun began to set, casting long shadows across the forest floor, the group made their way back to the lodge. JT's father and brother were in high spirits, regaling each other with stories of past hunts and the ones that got away. JT forced a smile, trying to fit in, but his eyes kept scanning the surroundings, searching for something that might explain the strange occurrences. That evening, as they sat down to a simple but hearty meal of stew and bread, the local guide, Jack, walked in, his usual boisterous demeanor noticeably subdued. He nodded curtly at the group, his eyes avoiding direct contact. "Hey, Jack," JT's father said, "how's the hunting been? We had a weird encounter today." Jack's gaze flickered towards JT before quickly looking away. "Oh, yeah? What kind of weird?" JT's father launched into a story about the buck in the clearing, but Jack's expression remained impassive. When the tale ended, Jack nodded thoughtfully, his voice low and serious. "You boys should head back to town before the full moon. This forest can get... funny around that time." Mike snorted. "Funny? What do you mean?" Jack's eyes darted towards the windows, as if he feared being overheard. "Just trust me, okay? It's not a good time to be out here." JT's father chuckled. "Come on, Jack, you're not going to start telling us ghost stories, are you?" Jack's face turned grim. "I'm telling you, it's not safe. You don't want to be out here when the moon's full." The group exchanged skeptical glances, but JT felt a shiver run down his spine. There was something in Jack's tone, a warning that went beyond mere superstition. As the night wore on, Jack's behavior became increasingly erratic. He excused himself early, citing fatigue, and JT noticed that he left his usual flask of whiskey behind. The group retired to their cabins, the darkness outside seeming to press in on them like a physical presence. JT lay in bed, his mind racing with questions. What was going on in these woods? Why was Jack acting so strangely? And what had really happened in that clearing? He couldn't shake the feeling that he was missing something, that there was a truth lurking just beyond the edge of his perception. As he drifted off to sleep, he thought he heard a faint rustling outside, like leaves being disturbed by something that didn't want to be seen. JT's heart quickened, and he threw off the covers, approaching the window cautiously. The moon was rising, casting an silver glow over the forest. JT's eyes scanned the trees, searching for any sign of movement. And then, he saw it: a pair of eyes, watching him from the darkness. JT's breath caught in his throat as the eyes vanished, leaving him wondering if he'd really seen them at all. The rest of the night was a blur of fragmented sleep and uneasy wakefulness. JT's dreams were filled with images of the forest, twisted and distorted, the trees looming over him like sentinels. When he finally emerged from his cabin the next morning, the world seemed different, the shadows deeper and more menacing. "Hey, JT," Mike said, as they gathered around the breakfast table, "you look like you've seen a ghost." JT forced a laugh. "Just didn't sleep well, that's all." But as he glanced out the window, he could have sworn he saw something moving, just beyond the treeline. Something that didn't want to be seen. The day's hunt was a somber affair, the group's usual banter and laughter subdued by Jack's warning. JT's father and brother seemed to be waiting for something to happen, their eyes scanning the forest with a mix of excitement and trepidation. JT, on the other hand, felt like he was walking through a nightmare, the trees closing in on him like a vise. He knew that he had to get out of these woods, to escape the creeping dread that was slowly consuming him. But as the sun began to set, casting long shadows across the forest floor, JT realized that they were running out of time. The full moon was rising, and with it, a sense of foreboding that threatened to engulf them all.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The continuation is competent but generic. It maintains JT's isolated perspective and dread, but the prose is flat, heavily reliant on clichés ("shiver ran down his spine," "breath caught in his throat," "long shadows across the forest floor" — used twice). The new guide character Jack is introduced as a stock "warning-giver" whose dialogue is wooden exposition. The pacing rushes through two days with little sensory grounding; scenes are summarized rather than lived. King's grounding realism is absent — no specific textures, brand names, small human details. The tension builds toward an unearned cliffhanger rather than a payoff; the "eyes at the window" beat is the most clichéd possible move. Dialogue is functional but stilted. Emotionally it's shallow, telling us JT's fear repeatedly rather than embodying it. Word count is under target (~700). It's coherent and readable, just unremarkable. + Fantasy-humour: Hell is Other Demons — Score: 53.7
Prompt:
Hell is Other Demons Write a day in the life short story. Lucifer morningstar's on again/ off again relationship with Lilith is currently in the "off again" stage, though the cycle may be coming around as Lilith has been stopping by for reasons other than purely violent ones, and Lucifer has been increasingly lonely. Write a day in the life of this power couple, with elements of dark comedy & fantasy. Use understated irony & absurdity by merging the black immorality and everyday business of running hell, against relatable domestic relationship drama. Take cues from Neil Gaiman. 1000 words.
Model Output:
Lucifer Morningstar sipped his coffee, gazing out the window of his penthouse office in the heart of Hell. The sulfurous skies were a deep, foreboding crimson, matching his mood. It had been weeks since Lilith last set foot in his domain, and the silence was starting to get to him. He missed her, despite the fact that their on-again, off-again relationship was a never-ending cycle of passion and destruction. Just as he was about to call for his assistant, Beezle, to bring him some fresh coffee, the door burst open and Lilith strode in, her long, raven-black hair a wild tangle around her face. She was carrying her signature scorched leather purse, adorned with strange trinkets and talismans pilfered from the mortal realms. "Morning, darling," she said, dropping the purse onto the coffee table with a clatter. "I brought you a little something." Lucifer raised an eyebrow as she rummaged through the purse, producing a small, taxidermied owl with a wonky eye. "A gift?" Lilith smiled, her eyes glinting with mischief. "I found it in a thrift store in the mortal realm. I thought it suited you." Lucifer couldn't help but chuckle, despite himself. "You know me too well." As they chatted, Beezle hovered at the door, clearing his throat to remind Lucifer of the day's schedule. "Ah, yes, the meeting with the Council of Infernal Affairs. I'll be right there." Lilith snorted. "You and your bureaucratic nonsense. I'll leave you to it." But instead of storming out, she lingered, fiddling with the owl's wonky eye. Lucifer's heart skipped a beat as he realized she was staying. Maybe this was the start of another cycle, another round of passion and chaos. The meeting with the Council was a tedious affair, with Lucifer fielding complaints about the recent influx of mortal souls and the subsequent strain on Hell's infrastructure. He listened patiently, nodding along as the various demons and imps presented their grievances. Meanwhile, Lilith wandered the halls of the palace, leaving a trail of minor destruction in her wake. She turned a fountain into a miniature whirlpool, summoned a swarm of biting flies to harass the palace guards, and rearranged the artwork in the gallery to spell out "LILITH WAS HERE" in bold, crimson letters. When Lucifer finally emerged from the meeting, he found her lounging on a nearby couch, flipping through a magazine. "You're being unusually... subtle today," he observed. Lilith looked up, a mischievous glint in her eye. "I'm just getting started, darling. I have a few more tricks up my sleeve." As the day wore on, Lucifer found himself growing more and more distracted. He couldn't concentrate on the paperwork, and his usual charm and charisma deserted him during a meeting with a group of prospective clients. All he could think about was Lilith, and the way she made him feel like he was the only being in the universe. It wasn't until they were alone in the palace, the sun dipping below the horizon, that Lucifer realized the true extent of his feelings. He turned to Lilith, his voice low and husky. "You know, I've been thinking... maybe it's time we started another cycle." Lilith raised an eyebrow, a slow smile spreading across her face. "Oh? And what makes you think I'm interested?" Lucifer shrugged, a wry smile twisting his lips. "I don't know. Maybe it's the way you're still carrying that ridiculous purse, or the fact that you're still leaving your mark on my palace. But I think we both know that we're not done with each other yet." Lilith's smile grew, and for a moment, Lucifer thought he saw a glimmer of something almost like affection in her eyes. "You're right, darling. We're not done yet. Not by a long shot." As the night wore on, the palace erupted into chaos, with flames licking at the walls and the sound of screams echoing through the halls. Lucifer and Lilith danced through the mayhem, their laughter and shouts mingling with the cacophony. It was just another day in the life of the power couple of Hell, where the boundaries between love and destruction were blissfully blurred.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece fulfills the basic brief: it's a day-in-the-life of Lucifer and Lilith in an "off-again" phase warming toward reconciliation, with some dark comedy (the taxidermied owl, the "LILITH WAS HERE" gallery vandalism) and domestic drama set against the mundane bureaucracy of Hell (Council of Infernal Affairs, infrastructure complaints). However, it executes these elements at a fairly generic level. Strengths: The premise of merging infernal business with relationship drama is present, and the owl gift is a nice small character beat. The prose is clean and readable, and the structure is coherent. Weaknesses: The piece leans heavily on cliché — "raven-black hair," "mischievous glint," "heart skipped a beat," "low and husky voice." The Gaiman influence is absent in texture; there's none of his dry wit, mythic resonance, or understated irony. The comedy is spelled out rather than embodied ("You and your bureaucratic nonsense"). The ending is rushed — the palace "erupting into chaos" with flames and screams is told, not shown, and feels like a summary of a more interesting story. The reconciliation is under-earned; Lucifer simply declares his feelings and Lilith agrees, with little tension or resistance. Lilith's destruction is described in a list-like fashion that flattens it. The dialogue is serviceable but predictable, full of familiar beats ("We're not done with each other yet"). The word count falls short of the ~1000-word seed. There's little genuine absurdity or understatement — the irony is mostly stated rather than enacted. + Historical fiction: Gladiator — Score: 52.8
Prompt:
Historical Fiction: Write a scene from a story set during the height of the Roman Empire, focusing on a slice of a day in the life of a gladiator. Do not write a combat scene. Use sensory details to capture the sights, sounds, and smells of ancient Rome. Explore the gladiator's thoughts and emotions. The story should also touch on the larger political and social issues of the time period. The piece should feel like a slice of a larger story. First person, past tense, 1000 words.
Model Output:
I woke before dawn, the darkness of the ludus still heavy around me. The air was thick with the smell of sweat, leather, and the acrid tang of metal. I lay on my pallet, listening to the sound of my fellow gladiators stirring, their murmurs and groans echoing off the stone walls. The lanista, our trainer, would be coming soon to rouse us for the day's training. I swung my legs over the side of the pallet and planted my feet firmly on the cold floor. My name is Marcus, and I'm a murmillo, a heavily armored gladiator with a helmet shaped like a fish. I've been a slave to the games for five years now, and I've learned to cherish the small comforts: a good night's sleep, a hot meal, a few moments of quiet before the chaos begins. As I stood up, I reached for the small, worn leather pouch that hung from a nail on the wall. Inside, I kept a few tokens: a silver coin my sister had given me before I was sold into slavery, a lock of my mother's hair, and a tiny, hand-carved wooden figurine of Mars, the god of war. I rubbed the figurine between my fingers, feeling the familiar ridges and grooves. It was a superstition, I knew, but I believed it brought me luck. The lanista, a grizzled old veteran named Gracchus, appeared in the doorway, his eyes scanning the room. "Up, up, you dogs! Today's training will be hard. We have a big show coming up, and the editor wants us to be sharp." I joined the line of gladiators shuffling toward the training area. The sun was rising over the ludus, casting a golden glow over the sandy floor and the rows of training equipment. I breathed in deeply, feeling the cool morning air fill my lungs. It was moments like these that I forgot I was a slave, forgot that my life was not my own. As we trained, I couldn't help but think about the rumors circulating through the ludus. The emperor, Domitian, was getting more and more erratic, ordering mass executions and imposing harsh penalties on anyone who crossed him. The games were becoming more brutal, more spectacular, as if the emperor was trying to distract the people from the troubles brewing in the empire. I had heard whispers of a new tax on the plebeians, one that would cripple the already struggling lower classes. The patricians, of course, would be exempt, their wealth and influence protecting them from the emperor's wrath. It was a recipe for disaster, and I wondered how long it would be before the people rose up. Gracchus put us through our paces, drilling us in the finer points of combat. I was paired with a new recruit, a young Thracian who was still learning the basics. I took him under my wing, showing him the proper way to hold a sword, how to move in the arena. He was eager, but clumsy, and I could tell he was going to get himself killed if he didn't shape up. After training, we were given a short break before lunch. I made my way to the small shrine we had set up in the corner of the ludus, dedicated to the gods of war and the underworld. I made an offering of a few grains of wheat and a libation of wine, asking for protection and strength in the coming games. As I stood before the shrine, I felt a hand on my shoulder. It was my friend, a retiarius named Lucius. "Marcus, have you heard the news?" he whispered. "What news?" "The emperor's announced a new series of games, the biggest and most extravagant yet. He's going to fill the Colosseum with wild beasts and gladiators, and the whole city's invited. It's going to be a spectacle like no other." I felt a shiver run down my spine. I had heard rumors of the emperor's plans, but to hear it confirmed was something else entirely. "When is it?" "In three weeks' time. We'll be the main attraction, of course. The murmillo and the retiarius, facing off against the best the empire has to offer." I nodded, my mind already racing with strategies and tactics. I knew I had to be sharp, had to be at the top of my game if I was going to survive. Lucius clapped me on the back. "Don't worry, Marcus. We'll make it through. We always do." I smiled, feeling a surge of camaraderie with my fellow gladiator. We were slaves, yes, but we were also warriors, and we would face whatever the games threw our way with courage and honor. As I walked back to the barracks, the sun beating down on my shoulders, I felt the weight of my armor and the burden of my fate. I was a gladiator, a tool of the empire, but I was also a man, with thoughts and feelings and desires. And I would hold on to that, no matter what the games threw my way.
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece fulfills the prompt's basic requirements: first person, past tense, a non-combat day-in-the-life scene, sensory details, some political context (Domitian's tyranny, taxes, class tensions), and it reads as a slice of a larger story. However, it does so in a thoroughly workmanlike, predictable way. Strengths: The prose is clear and competent, the voice is consistent throughout, and the structure is coherent. The tokens in the leather pouch (sister's coin, mother's hair, Mars figurine) are a nice touch that grounds the character emotionally. The historical details (murmillo, retiarius, lanista, ludus, shrine offerings) are accurate and unforced. Weaknesses: The piece is heavily tell-don't-show. Marcus repeatedly announces his emotions rather than letting them emerge from action: "I felt a shiver run down my spine," "I felt a surge of camaraderie," "I felt the weight of my armor and the burden of my fate." The political content is delivered as exposition dumps ("I had heard whispers of a new tax on the plebeians...") rather than woven into the scene. The dialogue is functional but flat — Lucius's lines exist purely to deliver plot information, and "Don't worry, Marcus. We'll make it through. We always do" is a stock line. The mentorship of the clumsy Thracian recruit is a familiar trope with no fresh angle. The ending, with its "courage and honor" resolve, is generic uplift that doesn't feel earned by anything that happened in the scene — nothing was risked or lost. The character arc within the piece is essentially flat: Marcus starts stoic and ends stoic. The imagery is serviceable but thin — "smell of sweat, leather, and the acrid tang of metal" and "golden glow" are stock phrases. There's little that surprises, little specificity that makes this gladiator distinct from any other fictional gladiator. The prose never becomes purple or overwrought; it's simply plain. Emotionally, the piece gestures at depth (slavery, mortality, family loss) but doesn't linger or complicate any of it. + Epistolary: The Lighthouse Keeper — Score: 52.2
Prompt:
The Lighthouse Keeper Epistolary Narrative: Write a story told entirely through a series of brief correspondences: telegrams or letters or emails between two characters. The correspondence should span several months or years, and reveal a gradually unfolding plot. Use distinct voices for each character, and include details that provide insight into their personalities and motivations. The story should build to an emotional climax, and the final letter should provide a satisfying resolution. The setting is a lighthouse keeper writing to his mother. He is working class and Scottish. He is struggling with the isolation of his posting. Write naturally and without cliches. 1000 words.
Model Output:
**Letter 1: February 10th, 2015** From: Angus MacLeod, Lighthouse Keeper To: Morag MacLeod, Mother Dear Mam, Hope this letter finds ye in good health. I cannae believe it's been three months since I started here at the lighthouse. The isolation's gettin' to me, but I'm managin'. The work's hard, but it's a good kind o' tired. The Fresnel lens is a beauty, and I've grown fond o' the rhythm o' the light's rotation. The winter's been harsh, though. Storms come in off the sea and rattle the tower somethin' fierce. I've had to learn to tie down the shutters and secure the lantern room door or the wind'd rip 'em off. The generator's been playin' up, too, and I've had to tinker with it till the wee hours to get it runnin' again. But it's the silence that gets to me most. No' a soul to talk to but the seagulls and the wind. I've taken to talkin' to the radio just to hear a human voice. Take care, Mam. Write soon. Your lovin' son, Angus **Letter 2: March 20th, 2015** From: Morag MacLeod, Mother To: Angus MacLeod, Lighthouse Keeper Dear Angus, I'm glad to hear ye're managein', but I can hear the loneliness in yer words. I remember when yer da was at sea, and I'd go weeks without hearin' from him. It's hard, but ye're a strong lad. Ye'll get through this. I've been thinkin' o' sendin' ye a wee care package – some o' yer favorite shortbread and maybe a book or two. Would ye like that? And I've got a wee surprise for ye: yer cousin, Eilidh, is comin' to visit me soon. She's got a new bairn, and I know ye'll be wantin' to meet the wee one. Keep yer chin up, laddie. The spring'll bring better weather, and maybe some company. With love, Mam **Letter 3: May 1st, 2015** From: Angus MacLeod, Lighthouse Keeper To: Morag MacLeod, Mother Dear Mam, Thanks for the package! The shortbread's gone already, and I've started on the book – it's a good yarn. Eilidh's visit sounds like a treat; I'm lookin' forward to hearin' all about the bairn. Spring's arrived, but it's brought its own challenges. The sea's gettin' rougher, and the light's gotten a bit wonky. I've had to climb the tower in the middle o' the night to adjust the optics. The rain's comin' in sideways, and I've had to batten down the hatches. But it's a good kind o' chaos – keeps me on me toes. The worst part, though, is the supply boat's been delayed. We're runnin' low on food and fuel, and I'm gettin' a wee bit worried. The radio's been cracklin' with warnings o' a big storm brewin' out at sea. Keep yer fingers crossed for me, Mam. I'll write soon. Love, Angus **Letter 4: June 15th, 2015** From: Morag MacLeod, Mother To: Angus MacLeod, Lighthouse Keeper Dear Angus, I've been worried sick about ye! The news said that storm was a monster – I'm just glad ye're safe. I can only imagine how frightening it must've been, stuck out there on the rock. I've been thinkin' o' ye every day, wonderin' if ye were okay. Eilidh and the bairn came to visit, and we all sent our love. We've been havin' a right good time, but it's not the same without ye. I've got some news that might cheer ye up, though: the lighthouse authority's talkin' about sendin' a relief keeper to give ye a break. Maybe ye can come home for a visit? We'd love to have ye. Take care, laddie. We're all thinkin' o' ye. With love, Mam **Letter 5: August 20th, 2015** From: Angus MacLeod, Lighthouse Keeper To: Morag MacLeod, Mother Dear Mam, I can hardly believe it – the relief keeper arrived last week, and I'm comin' home for a visit! I'm over the moon. The thought o' seein' ye, Eilidh, and the bairn has kept me goin' these past few months. The storm was a nightmare, but the lighthouse held strong. I'm proud o' the old girl – she's a bonnie beastie. The isolation's still gettin' to me, but knowin' I've got a break comin' has made all the difference. I'll be home soon, and we can catch up in person. I cannae wait to hear all about Eilidh's bairn and give ye a big hug. Love, Angus **Letter 6: September 10th, 2015** From: Morag MacLeod, Mother To: Angus MacLeod, Lighthouse Keeper Dear Angus, Welcome home, laddie! It's been far too long since we've had ye under our roof. We've got a big dinner planned, and Eilidh's brought the bairn to meet ye. Ye'll be spoilin' that wee one rotten, I can tell ye. I can see the weight o' the lighthouse liftin' off yer shoulders already. Ye look like a new man. We'll get ye back to yerself in no time. We'll talk about the future soon, but for now, just enjoy bein' home. Ye deserve it. With all my love, Mam
Judge Evaluation:
[Analysis] The piece fulfills the basic brief: six letters between Angus, a Scottish lighthouse keeper, and his mother Morag, spanning roughly seven months, with distinct (if thin) voices for each. The Scots dialect is present but inconsistently applied — Angus drops in and out of "cannae," "o'," "wee," and "bonnie beastie" in a way that feels decorative rather than lived-in, and phrases like "batten down the hatches" and "keep yer chin up" lean on cliché despite the prompt's explicit instruction to avoid them. The plot arc — isolation, storm, delayed supplies, relief keeper, homecoming — is serviceable but predictable, and the stakes never truly bite. The storm Angus survives happens entirely off-page between letters, robbing the story of its natural climax. The emotional trajectory resolves too easily: the relief keeper arrives conveniently, the visit is arranged without friction, and the final letter closes on unearned warmth. There's no real cost, no revelation, no gradual unfolding of anything surprising. The mother's mention of "we'll talk about the future soon" gestures at depth that never arrives. Characterisation is sketch-level. Angus is "lonely but stoic," Morag is "warm and worrying." The cousin Eilidh and the bairn are mentioned but purely functional. The letters tell us emotions ("the isolation's gettin' to me," "I can hear the loneliness in yer words") rather than revealing them through detail, which is a classic tell-don't-show failure. There are a few genuinely nice touches — talking to the radio to hear a human voice, the "good kind o' tired" — but they're surrounded by stock phrasing. The prose is clean and readable, sentences flow adequately, and the epistolary form is maintained competently. But the piece reads as a competent exercise rather than a story with anything at stake, and the ending's tidy positivity isn't fully earned by what precedes it.
